# TIMELINE · Kali Yuga — Documented History

> **Period 7 of 9 on the cosmic clock.** One slice of the corpus-wide [TIMELINE hub](../TIMELINE.md); each entry links to the file where the event is detailed. Back to the [master index](../README.md).
> ← Prev: [Kali (Scriptural)](05-kali-yuga-scriptural.md) · [↑ TIMELINE hub](../TIMELINE.md) · Next: [Future Manvantaras](07-future-manvantaras.md) →

**1539 events.**

*The academic/historical timeline (flagged distinct from scripture).*

---

- **"Call your son to the table"** — the Bhairava refuses to eat unless a child of the house joins him, so the mother goes to the door and calls Cīrāḷaṉ by name exactly as on any other day _(actors: Śiva as Bhairava, Veṇkāṭṭu Nangai)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/siruthondar-nayanar.md) — (Periya Purāṇam, Ciṟuttoṇṭa Purāṇam)
- **"Elders full of Udayana tales"** — Kālidāsa's Meghadūta finds at Avantī the village elders skilled in the tales of Udayana, proof the romance was already proverbial by the 4th–5th c. CE _(actors: Kālidāsa, the cloud-messenger)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/udayana-vasavadatta.md) — (Meghadūta, Pūrvamegha)
- **"I will go tomorrow"** — longing to see Naṭarāja at Chidambaram yet held back by awe and unworthiness, Nandanar keeps deferring the pilgrimage with "nāḷai-p-pōvēṉ," earning the name Tirunāḷaippōvār _(actors: Nandanar)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/nandanar-tirunalaippovar.md) — (Periya Purāṇam, Tirunāḷaippōvār Nāyaṉār Purāṇam)
- **"Kaṇikaṇṇaṉ pōha" — the rolled-up serpent-bed** — banished for his disciple's sake, Tirumaḻiśai Āḻvār bids the reclining Lord of Tiruveḵkā roll up His snake-couch and follow, and Viṣṇu obeys, earning the epithet Yathoktakāri _(actors: Tirumaḻiśai Āḻvār, Kaṇikaṇṇaṉ, Yathoktakāri Viṣṇu, a Pallava king)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/alvar-hagiographies-guruparampara.md) — (Guruparampara Prabhāvam 6000-paḍi)
- **"One of only two Sarasvatī temples"** — Basara's fame rests on its rarity, styled the southern counterpart of Kashmir's Śāradā Pīṭha (or Śṛṅgerī's Śāradāmbā) _(actors: Sarasvatī/Śāradā)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/basara-gnana-saraswati-vyasa.md) — (temple tradition) [disputed]
- **'Adharaṃ Madhuram' — the sweetness refrain** — Each of the eight stanzas closes on *madhurādhipater akhilaṃ madhuram*, 'everything about the Lord of Sweetness/Mathurā is sweet,' the hymn's fixed anaphoric device _(actors: Vallabhācārya)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/stotras/madhurashtakam.md) — (Madhurāṣṭakam, refrain)
- **'Summā iru' — initiation into silence** — Murugan silences the overwhelmed poet with 'Be still, beyond words', a wordless initiation memorialised in Kandar Anubhūti v. 4 _(actors: Murugan, Aruṇagirinātar)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/arunagirinathar-tiruppugazh.md) — (Kandar Anubhūti, v. 4)
- **Abhinanda's Laghu-Yoga-Vāsiṣṭha abridgement (c. 9th–10th c. CE)** — The Kashmiri paṇḍit Abhinanda condenses the work to ~6,000 verses, the form in which it traveled most widely [scholarly] _(actors: Abhinanda of Kashmir)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/yoga-vasishtha.md) — (Laghu-Yoga-Vāsiṣṭha)
- **Abhinavagupta completes the Tantrāloka and Tantrasāra** — 37 āhnikas (~5,800 verses) synthesising Āgama, Spanda and Pratyabhijñā on the base of the Mālinīvijayottara, with his own prose digest (c. 950–1016 CE [scholarly]) _(actors: Abhinavagupta)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/kashmir-shaiva-texts.md) — (Tantrāloka 1; Tantrasāra)
- **Acchoda lake in Bāṇa's Kādambarī** — Bāṇabhaṭṭa sets Mahāśvetā's hermitage on the luminous Acchoda lake, giving the Purāṇic clear-water lake a celebrated literary afterlife (7th c. CE) [scholarly] _(actors: Bāṇabhaṭṭa, Mahāśvetā)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/acchoda-satyavati-rebirth.md) — (Kādambarī)
- **Achyuta Menon edits the Ballads of North Malabar (1935)** — C. Achyuta Menon collects and publishes the oral Vaṭakkan Pāṭṭukaḷ as Ballads of North Malabar (University of Madras, 2 vols.), the standard critical gathering that fixed the corpus's received shape _(actors: C. Achyuta Menon)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/vadakkan-pattukal-northern-ballads.md) — (Ballads of North Malabar, 1935)
- **Ādi Śaṅkara organizes the Daśanāmī ekadaṇḍi order** — the ten-named single-staff renunciate order with four cardinal maṭhas is traditionally founded (8th c. CE) _(actors: Ādi Śaṅkara, Daśanāmī sannyāsins)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/sannyasa-and-the-renunciate-tradition.md) — (Daśanāmī maṭha tradition)
- **Adi Shankara Organizes the Greater Char Dham** — Adi Shankara (c. 8th c. CE) is traditionally credited with organizing the four pan-India abodes (Badrinath, Dwarka, Puri, Rameswaram) into one pilgrimage to bind the subcontinent _(actors: Adi Shankara)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/char-dham-and-chota-char-dham.md) — (Wikipedia, Char Dham)
- **Adi Shankara Renounces as a Boy at Kaladi** — Born at Kaladi to an aged childless couple, the precocious Shankara renounces as a boy after a crocodile seizes his leg, forcing his mother's consent to sannyasa _(actors: Adi Shankara, Aryamba)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/adi-shankara.md) — (Madhaviya Shankara-Digvijaya)
- **Adoption by the Vaṇiya household** — the Vaṇiya headman Muchilōṭṭu Paḍanāyar and his wife recognize her divinity and install her, making her the kuladevata of the oil-presser community _(actors: the Bhagavatī, Muchilōṭṭu Paḍanāyar)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/muchilottu-bhagavathy.md) — (Muchilottu Bhagavathy thoṭṭam-pāṭṭu; Kannur kāvu tradition)
- **Adoption of Dali Bai** — Ramdev finds and adopts the Meghwal (Dalit) girl Dali Bai as his sister; she becomes his foremost devotee and the cult's anti-untouchability charter _(actors: Ramdev, Dali Bai)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/ramdev-pir.md) — (Ramdev hagiographic tradition) [folk]
- **Afzal Khan desecrates the shrine (1659 CE)** — the Bijapur general raids Tuljapur before marching on Shivaji; the goddess assures victory, fulfilled at Pratapgad on 10 Nov 1659 _(actors: Afzal Khan, Shivaji, Bhavānī)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/tuljapur-tulja-bhavani.md) — (Maratha bakhar tradition) [regional] [folk]
- **Afzal Khan's raid on Tuljapur and Pratapgad (1659 CE)** — the Bijapur general desecrates the Tuljapur shrine (the true mūrti hidden, in the devotional telling) and is killed at Pratapgad on 10 Nov 1659, read by tradition as Bhavānī's assured victory _(actors: Afzal Khan, Shivaji, Tuljā Bhavānī)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/tulja-bhavani.md) — (Maratha bakhar tradition; battle dating [scholarly]) [regional] [folk]
- **Agama Hindu Bali wins state recognition; Parisada founded (1958–1959)** — Balinese leaders codify Sang Hyang Widhi Wasa monotheism and the Panca Sradha to gain recognition under Pancasila, creating the Parisada Hindu Dharma council [scholarly] _(actors: Balinese religious leaders, Parisada Hindu Dharma, Indonesian Ministry of Religion)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/balinese-hinduism-agama-hindu-dharma.md) — (modern historiography, Picard)
- **Ahobila Maṭha founded** — Ādivaṇ Śaṭhakopa Jīyar establishes the Vaḍakalai seat in 1398, initiated per tradition by Narasiṃha of Ahobilam himself [traditional] _(actors: Ādivaṇ Śaṭhakopa Jīyar, Narasiṃha)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/vedanta-desika-and-later-srivaishnava-acharyas.md) — (Wikipedia, Ahobila Mutt)
- **Ajātaśatru's survey of the six heretical teachers** — King Ajātaśatru of Magadha reports to the Buddha that he had previously questioned each of the six rival śramaṇa teachers (Pūraṇa Kassapa, Pakudha Kaccāyana, Sañjaya Belaṭṭhaputta, Ajita Kesakambali, Nigaṇṭha Nātaputta/Mahāvīra, Makkhali Gosāla) about the visible fruits of the ascetic life and found their answers unsatisfying — the canonical doxographic frame of the 6th–5th c. BCE heterodox field _(actors: Ajātaśatru, the Buddha, Pūraṇa Kassapa, Pakudha Kaccāyana, Sañjaya Belaṭṭhaputta, Ajita Kesakambali, Nigaṇṭha Nātaputta (Mahāvīra), Makkhali Gosāla)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/nastika-darshanas-and-the-astika-nastika-frame.md) — (Sāmaññaphala Sutta, Dīgha Nikāya 2)
- **Ajmal's plunge at Dwarka and Krishna's boon** — the childless Ajmal Tanwar of Pokaran leaps into the sea at Dwarka and Krishna promises to be born in his house _(actors: Ajmal Tanwar, Krishna-Dvarakanatha, Mainade)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/ramdev-pir.md) — (Ramdev hagiographic tradition) [folk]
- **Akṣara-abhyāsam at Basara** — families across the southern Deccan bring toddlers to trace their first letters before Jñāna Sarasvatī, the regional form of the Vidyārambha saṃskāra _(actors: pilgrims, priests)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/basara-gnana-saraswati-vyasa.md) — (temple practice)
- **Akṣobhya Tīrtha's *tat tvam asi* debate at Mulbāgal** — Madhva's direct disciple debates the Advaitin Vidyāraṇya with Vedānta Deśika as arbiter, the verdict-verse declaring Akṣobhya victor, 14th c. CE [traditional] [disputed] _(actors: Akṣobhya Tīrtha, Vidyāraṇya, Vedānta Deśika)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/raghavendra-tirtha-and-the-dvaita-line.md) — (Dvaita matha tradition; Sharma, History of the Dvaita School)
- **Āḻvār bridal mysticism anticipates madhurya** — Nammāḻvār's nāyakī-bhāva and Āṇḍāḷ's bridal poems pour devotion into Tamil akam love-conventions centuries before Rūpa (c. 7th–9th c. CE [scholarly]) _(actors: Nammāḻvār, Āṇḍāḷ)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/bhakti-rasa-and-the-bhavas.md) — (Nālāyira Divya Prabandham, Nācciyār Tirumoḻi)
- **Ambedkar burns the Manusmṛti (1927)** — during the Mahad Satyāgraha B. R. Ambedkar publicly burns the code as the charter of caste hierarchy and untouchability, commemorated as Manusmṛti Dahan Din _(actors: B. R. Ambedkar, Mahad Satyāgraha)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/manusmriti-map.md) — (documented history, 25 Dec 1927)
- **Angkor turns Buddhist under Jayavarman VII** — the Vaiṣṇava temple-mountain city is superseded by Mahāyāna Angkor Thom and the Bayon, and Angkor Wat itself becomes a Theravāda shrine _(actors: Jayavarman VII)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/greater-india-sacred-geography.md) — ([scholarly] c. 1181–1218 CE)
- **Angkor Wat built as Vishnuloka** — Sūryavarman II raises the largest religious monument on earth, a moated Meru dedicated to Viṣṇu and named Parama-viṣṇuloka, in the Khmer capital Yaśodharapura _(actors: Sūryavarman II, Viṣṇu)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/greater-india-sacred-geography.md) — (Khmer inscriptions; [scholarly] c. 1113–1150 CE)
- **Appar's ordeals and the Pallava king's conversion (7th c. CE)** — the ex-Jain monk Dharmasēna survives lime-kiln, poison, elephant and drowning, converting Mahendravarman I [scholarly] _(actors: Appar/Tirunāvukkaracar, Tilakavatī, Mahendravarman I)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/periya-puranam-63-nayanmar-legend-map.md) — (Periya Purāṇam, Tirunāvukkaracar Purāṇam)
- **Appayya Dīkṣita's 104 works** — the 16th-c. Śaiva polymath of Adayapalam writes the Advaita doxography Siddhānta-leśa-saṅgraha, the Śiva-Advaita Śivārka-maṇi-dīpikā, and the poetics manual Kuvalayānanda under Cinna Bomma Nāyaka's patronage _(actors: Appayya Dīkṣita, Cinna Bomma Nāyaka)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/later-advaita-acharyas.md) — (Siddhānta-leśa-saṅgraha; Wikipedia) [traditional]
- **Ārōmaluṇṇi avenges his father** — Ārōmal's posthumous son, reared and drilled in the kaḷari by the Puttūram elders, grows into a champion and hunts down and kills the traitor Cāntu, redeeming the family's blood-debt _(actors: Ārōmaluṇṇi, Cāntu)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/vadakkan-pattukal-northern-ballads.md) — (Vaṭakkan Pāṭṭukaḷ, Ārōmaluṇṇi pāṭṭu)
- **Aruṇagiri's ruin** — a dissolute youth of Tiruvaṇṇāmalai squanders his family's wealth on courtesans and, diseased and destitute, is broken by shame at his sister Adhi's devotion _(actors: Aruṇagirinātar, his sister Adhi)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/arunagirinathar-tiruppugazh.md) — (Aruṇagirinātar hagiographic tradition, 15th c.)
- **Aśvaghoṣa composes the earliest surviving mahākāvyas** — the Buddhacarita and Saundarananda show ornate court epic already mature, c. 1st–2nd c. CE [scholarly] _(actors: Aśvaghoṣa)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/classical-kavya-and-sanskrit-drama.md) — (Buddhacarita, colophons)
- **Āyurveda adopts the Brahma-muhūrta rising rule** — the dinacaryā chapters prescribe rising in Brahma-muhūrta 'to protect the life-span', classical Āyurvedic compendia c. 600 CE [scholarly] _(actors: Vāgbhaṭa)_ → [details](../00-time-and-cosmology/muhurta-and-auspicious-timing.md) — (Aṣṭāṅga Hṛdaya, Sūtrasthāna 2.1)
- **Ayya Vaikundar inaugurates Ayyavazhi** — After the 1833 Tiruchendur sea-bath tradition holds transformed him into the avatar-son of Nārāyaṇa, Vaikundar preaches caste equality and the coming Dharma Yukam from Swamithope, his teaching fixed in the Akilattirattu Ammanai (1841) _(actors: Ayya Vaikundar; Hari Gopalan Citar; Swathi Thirunal of Travancore)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/modern-teachers-and-reform.md) — (Akilattirattu Ammanai; Wikipedia (Ayya Vaikundar, Ayyavazhi)) [regional] [folk]
- **Baitāl Pacīsī and the birth of literary Hindi** — Ṣūrat Kabīshwar's Braj rendering (1719–49) is turned into Hindustani by Lallū Lāl under John Gilchrist in 1805, a foundational text of literary Hindi [scholarly] _(actors: Ṣūrat Kabīshwar, Lallū Lāl, John Gilchrist)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/vetala-panchavimshati-vikram-and-betal.md) — (Baitāl Pacīsī, 1805)
- **Balarāma Dāsa composes the Odia Lakṣmī Purāṇa** — the eldest of the Pañcasakhā writes the vrata-charter poem of Lakṣmī and Śriyā in early 16th-century Purī, a founding text of caste-critique bhakti [scholarly] _(actors: Balarāma Dāsa, the Pañcasakhā)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/lakshmi-purana-shriya-chandaluni.md) — (Lakṣmī Purāṇa of Balarāma Dāsa; Odia literary histories)
- **Balarāma Dāsa writes the Jagamohana (Daṇḍī) Rāmāyaṇa and Lakṣmī Purāṇa** — the first Odia Rāmāyaṇa and the caste-critique poem chartering the Māṇabasā Gurubāra vrata, early 16th century CE [scholarly] _(actors: Balarāma Dāsa, Lakṣmī, Jagannātha)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/odia-literature-sarala-and-panchasakha.md) — (Jagamohana Rāmāyaṇa; Lakṣmī Purāṇa)
- **Bāmākhepā Born at Āṭlā** — the future cremation-saint Bāmācaraṇ Caṭṭopādhyāy is born (1837) at midnight on Śivarātri in Āṭlā village, Birbhum, near Tārāpīṭha _(actors: Bāmākhepā)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/tarapith-tara-vashishtha-bamakhepa.md) — (Wikipedia, Bamakhepa) [scholarly]
- **Bāmākhepā's Cremation-Ground Sādhanā** — under Kailāśpati Bābā, Bāmākhepā perfects yoga and Tantric practice living in Tārāpīṭha's mahāśmaśāna and 'sweetens' the fierce Ugra-Tārā into an intimate nursing Mother _(actors: Bāmākhepā, Kailāśpati Bābā, Tārā)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/tarapith-tara-vashishtha-bamakhepa.md) — (academia.edu, The Sweetening of Death) [scholarly]
- **Bāmākhepā's Samādhi (1911)** — Bāmākhepā dies in 1911 and his samādhi is set within the Tārāpīṭha complex beside the burning-ground, making the pīṭha a living saint-shrine worshipped alongside Tārā _(actors: Bāmākhepā, Tārā)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/tarapith-tara-vashishtha-bamakhepa.md) — (Wikipedia, Bamakhepa) [scholarly]
- **Bandhu Mohanty arrives too late** — the poor devotee, having saved for years to offer bhoga, reaches Purī after the deities are put to rest for the night and sits weeping at the shut temple, his lifetime's hope undone _(actors: Bandhu Mohanty, Jagannātha)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/characters/jagannatha-bhakta-legends.md) — (Odia bhakta-caritra) [oral tradition] [folk]
- **Basara temple attributed to a Deccan king** — the built Jñāna Sarasvatī shrine is raised in the early-medieval centuries, traditionally by a Karnataka/Chalukya ruler named Bijjaḷa _(actors: Bijjaḷa/Bijaludu)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/basara-gnana-saraswati-vyasa.md) — (local history) [disputed]
- **Batu Caves dedicated to Murugan** — the trader K. Thamboosamy Pillai develops the Kuala Lumpur cave-temple that becomes the global focus of Thaipūsam kāvaḍi, later crowned by a 42.7 m Murugan statue _(actors: K. Thamboosamy Pillai, Murugan)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/greater-india-sacred-geography.md) — ([scholarly] c. 1890; statue 2006)
- **Baṭuk Bhairav in Nepal and North India** — the boy-form spreads as a Newar Kathmandu-Valley guardian and a North Indian household Bhairav Bābā, propitiated on Kālāṣṭamī and Bhairavāṣṭamī _(actors: Baṭuka Bhairava, Newar and North Indian devotees)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/vatuka-bhairava-origin.md) — (regional cult tradition)
- **Belanjong pillar erected at Sanur (914 CE)** — earliest dated Balinese inscription, in Sanskrit and Old Balinese, attesting an Indic royal cult on Bali [scholarly] _(actors: Śrī Kesarī Warmadewa)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/balinese-hinduism-agama-hindu-dharma.md) — (Belanjong pillar inscription)
- **Bengal Sati Regulation XVII abolishes satī (4 Dec 1829)** — Governor-General Bentinck declares the burning or burying alive of widows culpable homicide after Rām Mohan Roy's campaign; the orthodox Privy Council appeal is dismissed in 1832 [scholarly] _(actors: Lord William Bentinck, Rām Mohan Roy, Mṛtyuñjaya Vidyālaṅkāra)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/sati-jauhar-and-the-immolation-rites.md) — (Bengal Sati Regulation XVII, 1829)
- **Bhadrācala Rāmadāsu builds the Rāma temple and is imprisoned** — The tahsildar Kancherla Gopanna builds the Bhadrāchalam Rāma temple with diverted revenue and, imprisoned ~12 years in Golconda fort, composes his anguished Rāma-kīrtanas; legend has Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa repay the sultan Tānīśā to free him _(actors: Bhadrācala Rāmadāsu (Kancherla Gopanna); Abul Hasan Qutb Shah (Tānīśā))_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/haridasa-and-carnatic-bhakti.md) — (Dāśarathī śatakam; Wikipedia (Bhadrachala Ramadasu)) [scholarly] [folk]
- **Bhadravarman founds Bhadreśvara at My Son** — the Cham king consecrates the Śaiva dynastic sanctuary of Champa, fusing his own name with Śiva as the liṅga-cult Bhadreśvara _(actors: Bhadravarman I, Śiva)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/greater-india-sacred-geography.md) — (Cham Sanskrit inscriptions; [scholarly] late 4th c. CE)
- **Bhāgyacandra stages the first Manipuri Rāsa-līlā** — the Meitei king choreographs Kṛṣṇa's rāsa from a dream-vision for the Govindajī temple (1779 CE) [regional] _(actors: Maharaja Bhāgyacandra, Kṛṣṇa in vision)_ → [details](../09-artifacts-symbols-and-arts/classical-dance-forms-and-ritual-theatre.md) — (Cheitharol Kumbaba chronicle tradition)
- **Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu completed at Vṛndāvana** — Rūpa Gosvāmī finishes the four-division 'ocean' that makes Kṛṣṇa-rati itself the sthāyi-bhāva and bhakti the supreme rasa (Śaka 1463 = 1541 CE, per colophon) _(actors: Rūpa Gosvāmī)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/bhakti-rasa-and-the-bhavas.md) — (Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu, colophon)
- **Bhaktiranna's cloud-chariot return** — hearing of the heroes' deaths at Kasi, the pilgrim uncle Bhaktiranna returns in a cloud-chariot, frees the confined Papanuka, orders sati on the Yadava women and performs pinda offerings on the Paleru _(actors: Bhaktiranna, Papanuka)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/katamaraju-katha.md) — (Katamaraju Kathalu, martyr-ending version)
- **Bhāṇḍu Datta's treachery and Kālketu's capture** — The corrupt kāyastha Bhāṇḍu Datta instigates the Kālinga king to invade Gujarāṭ; Kālketu is captured, then freed when Caṇḍī commands the king in a dream _(actors: Bhāṇḍu Datta, Kālketu, the Kālinga king, Caṇḍī)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/chandimangal-kalketu-and-srimanta.md) — (Mukundarāma, Caṇḍīmaṅgal, Ākheṭika khaṇḍa)
- **Bhānudās restores the image from Vijayanagara (16th c.)** — Vārkarī hagiography tells that the Viṭṭhala image, carried to Vijayanagara, was brought back to Pandharpur by Bhānudās, great-grandfather of Eknāth _(actors: Bhānudās, Viṭṭhala image, Vijayanagara emperor)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/pandharpur-vitthala-wari.md) — (Mahīpati, Bhaktavijaya) [folk] [disputed]
- **Bharata's rasa-sūtra codified** — the Nāṭya Śāstra defines rasa as arising from vibhāva, anubhāva and vyabhicāri-bhāva, the secular apparatus later transposed onto devotion (c. 200 BCE–200 CE [scholarly]) _(actors: Bharata Muni)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/bhakti-rasa-and-the-bhavas.md) — (Nāṭya Śāstra, ch. 6–7)
- **Bhāratcandra composes the Annadāmaṅgal (1752)** — the poet Bhāratcandra Rāy "Rāy-Guṇākar" completes the last and most polished Bengali maṅgal-kāvya at the Nadia court, glorifying the goddess Annadā (Annapūrṇā) _(actors: Bhāratcandra Rāy, Rāja Kṛṣṇacandra of Nadia, Annadā)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/annadamangal-bharatchandra.md) — (Annadāmaṅgal, colophon)
- **Bhāravi composes the Kirātārjunīya** — 18-canto mahākāvya on Arjuna's duel with Śiva-as-hunter, c. 6th c. CE [scholarly] _(actors: Bhāravi)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/classical-kavya-and-sanskrit-drama.md) — (Kirātārjunīya 1–18)
- **Bhāsa dramatizes the saga** — the Trivandrum plays attributed to Bhāsa stage the capture in Pratijñāyaugandharāyaṇa and the fire-ruse and dream-scene in Svapnavāsavadatta, his acknowledged masterpiece _(actors: Bhāsa, Udayana, Vāsavadattā, Yaugandharāyaṇa)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/udayana-vasavadatta.md) — (Bhāsa, Svapnavāsavadatta & Pratijñāyaugandharāyaṇa)
- **Bhāsa's plays composed** — thirteen dramas reshaping epic material (Ūrubhaṅga's dying Duryodhana, Karṇabhāra, Pañcarātra's averted war), dated anywhere from c. 2nd c. BCE to the early centuries CE [scholarly] [disputed] _(actors: Bhāsa)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/classical-kavya-and-sanskrit-drama.md) — (Trivandrum Sanskrit Series; Mālavikāgnimitra, prastāvanā)
- **Bhāskararāya completes the Saubhāgya-bhāskara (c. 1728 CE)** — the great Śrī-Vidyā systematizer (1690–1785) writes the standard commentary on the thousand names, fixing the received text and its Śrī-cakra/Pañcadaśī reading [scholarly] _(actors: Bhāskararāya Makhin)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/stotras/lalita-sahasranama.md) — (Saubhāgya-bhāskara)
- **Bhaṭṭi composes the Bhaṭṭikāvya at Valabhī** — 22-canto Rāvaṇavadha telling the Rāmāyaṇa while exemplifying Pāṇini's grammar, c. 7th c. CE [scholarly] _(actors: Bhaṭṭi, Śrīdharasena)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/classical-kavya-and-sanskrit-drama.md) — (Bhaṭṭikāvya 1–22, colophon)
- **Bhavabhūti stages the Uttararāmacarita** — court poet of Yaśovarman of Kanyākubja (early 8th c. CE [scholarly]) rewrites the Sītā-abandonment with a reunion happy ending via Vālmīki's play-within-a-play _(actors: Bhavabhūti, Yaśovarman)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/classical-kavya-and-sanskrit-drama.md) — (Uttararāmacarita 1–7)
- **Bhavānanda aids Mān Siṃha (c. 1600 CE)** — the goddess's devotee Bhavānanda Majumdār supports the Mughal general Mān Siṃha's campaign against the Bengal rebel Pratāpāditya of Jessore _(actors: Bhavānanda Majumdār, Rāja Mān Siṃha, Pratāpāditya)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/annadamangal-bharatchandra.md) — (Annadāmaṅgal, Mānsiṃha khaṇḍa)
- **Bhoja unearths Vikramāditya's throne** — reports of a mound that made a cowherd judge like a king lead the Paramāra Bhoja of Dhārā to excavate the buried siṃhāsana borne on thirty-two statuettes and attempt to be enthroned _(actors: Bhoja Paramāra, a cowherd, the 32 statuettes)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/simhasana-dvatrimshika-vikramaditya-cycle.md) — (Siṃhāsana-dvātriṃśikā, frame)
- **Biroba enthroned at Ārevāḍī** — the shepherd-god's great cult-centre rises at Ārevāḍī in Sāngli district, focus of the Dhangar Biroba ovī and vigil-worship _(actors: Biroba, the Dhangar community)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/dhangar-oral-epics-biroba-mhaskoba-dhuloba.md) — (Biroba ovī; Sontheimer, Pastoral Deities)
- **Birth of Tejaji at Kharnal** — the Jat hero Vir Teja is born to Taharji and Ramkunwari of the Dhauliya clan, by folk reckoning in V.S. 1130 (1074 CE) [folk] [disputed] _(actors: Tejaji, Taharji, Ramkunwari)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/tejaji.md) — (Tejaji katha, Kharnal shrine tradition)
- **Birth of Tulsīdās** — the future poet-saint is born (trad. V.S. 1589 / 1532 CE, birth-year disputed) at Rājāpur under the inauspicious Abhuktamūla nakṣatra, speaking "Rāma" as his first word _(actors: Tulsīdās, Ātmārām Dūbe, Hulsī)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/tulsidas.md) — (Mūla Gosāīṃ Carita [late text]; Wikipedia, Tulsidas)
- **Birth of Vedānta Deśika at Tūppul** — Veṅkaṭanātha, the future kavi-tārkika-siṃha, is born near Kāñcīpuram in 1268 [traditional] _(actors: Vedānta Deśika, Ātreya Rāmānuja)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/vedanta-desika-and-later-srivaishnava-acharyas.md) — (Wikipedia, Vedanta Desika)
- **Blackburn documents the bow-song tradition** — Stuart Blackburn's late-1970s Tirunelveli fieldwork and Singing of Birth and Death (1988) record the vil pāṭṭu genre, its birth-vs-death story types and the Muttuppaṭṭaṉ Katai in English _(actors: Stuart Blackburn, bow-song singers)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/muttuppattan-katai.md) — (Blackburn, Singing of Birth and Death, 1988) [scholarly]
- **Bodāṇā's tulsī yātrā** — the aged Ḍākor Rājpūt Bodāṇā walks to Dvārakā twice a year carrying holy basil for Raṇchhoḍrāī, keeping the vow into his seventies _(actors: Bodāṇā, Gaṅgābāī, Raṇchhoḍrāī)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/dakor-ranchhodrai-bodana.md) — (Ḍākor Māhātmya)
- **Brahma-vaivarta Rādhā-tattva codified** — the Brahma-vaivarta Purāṇa in its received form (c. 15th–16th c. CE) gives Rādhā her full cosmic theology of Goloka, the Pañca-prakṛti and supremacy over Vaikuṇṭha _(actors: Rādhā, Kṛṣṇa)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/radha.md) — (Brahma-vaivarta Purāṇa) [scholarly dating; late text]
- **Buddhabhatta composes the Ratnapariksha** — The fullest classical Sanskrit lapidary carrying the Bala gem-origin myth is composed in the mid-first millennium CE (Gupta horizon), later edited by Louis Finot (1896) [scholarly] _(actors: Buddhabhatta)_ → [details](../08-beings-and-bestiary/bala-asura-navaratna-origin.md) — (Buddhabhatta, Ratnapariksha; Finot, Les lapidaires indiens)
- **Budhasvāmin's Bṛhatkathāślokasaṃgraha composed** — a Nepalese-transmitted, first-person verse epitome of the lost Bṛhatkathā (c. 8th–9th c. CE, date disputed), lacking the Kailāsa frame and argued by Lacôte to stand closest to Guṇāḍhya's original _(actors: Budhasvāmin)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/brihatkatha-kathasaritsagara-and-vikramaditya-cycle.md) — (Bṛhatkathāślokasaṃgraha) [scholarly]
- **Burzōy's Pahlavi translation** — the physician Burzōy carries the Panchatantra into Middle Persian at the Sasanian court, c. 550 CE [scholarly], parent of the lost Pahlavi and surviving Syriac versions _(actors: Burzōy, Khosrow I Anūshirvān)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/panchatantra-hitopadesha-and-fable-literature.md) — (Kalilag and Damnag transmission history)
- **Caitanya instructs Rūpa at Prayāga** — ten days of teaching at Daśāśvamedha-ghāṭ plant the seed of bhakti-rasa doctrine and dispatch Rūpa to Vṛndāvana (1516 CE [late text]) _(actors: Caitanya Mahāprabhu, Rūpa Gosvāmī)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/bhakti-rasa-and-the-bhavas.md) — (Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Madhya-līlā 19)
- **Caitanya re-identifies Rādhā-kuṇḍa** — on his Braj pilgrimage c. 1515 CE, Caitanya Mahāprabhu locates the lost Rādhā-kuṇḍa in paddy-fields, and his Gosvāmī disciples systematize the hlādinī-śakti theology of Rādhā _(actors: Caitanya Mahāprabhu, Six Gosvāmīs)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/radha.md) — (Gauḍīya tradition) [scholarly]
- **Caitanya relishes Vidyāpati's padas at Purī** — in the 16th century Caitanya Mahāprabhu tastes Vidyāpati's songs day and night alongside Jayadeva and Caṇḍīdāsa, launching the Brajabuli imitation-corpus under the poet's signature _(actors: Caitanya Mahāprabhu, Svarūpa Dāmodara, Rāmānanda Rāya)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/ugna-mahadev-vidyapati.md) — (Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Madhya 2.77)
- **Calon Arang unleashes plague under Airlangga (setting: 11th c.; oldest MS 1540 CE)** — the widow-witch of Girah worships Durgā and ravages the kingdom until the sage Mpu Bharadah defeats her, the tale behind Bali's Rangda mask [regional] _(actors: Calon Arang, Ratna Manggali, Mpu Bharadah, Mpu Bahula, Airlangga)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/balinese-hinduism-agama-hindu-dharma.md) — (Calon Arang, Old Javanese prose)
- **Caṇḍeśa canonized as the 20th Nāyaṉār** — named in Sundarar's Tiruttoṇṭattokai and versified by Cēkkiḻār as the 20th of the 63 Nāyaṉār lives in the Periya Purāṇam (c. 1135 CE) _(actors: Caṇḍeśa, Cuntarar/Sundarar, Cēkkiḻār)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/characters/chandesvara-chandikeshvara.md) — (Tiruttoṇṭattokai; Periya Purāṇam, Tirumuṟai 12)
- **Caṇḍī rescues Śrīmanta at the execution-ground** — Śrīmanta, condemned in Ceylon like his father, is saved when Caṇḍī's host routs the king's men; the king worships her, is granted the Kamale-Kāminī vision, frees Dhanapati and weds Suśīlā to Śrīmanta _(actors: Śrīmanta, Caṇḍī, the Siṁhala king, Dhanapati, Suśīlā)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/chandimangal-kalketu-and-srimanta.md) — (Mukundarāma, Caṇḍīmaṅgal, Bāṇik khaṇḍa)
- **Caṇḍī reveals herself as the golden iguana to Kālketu** — The goddess, caught by the hunter Kālketu as a golden monitor-lizard tied with his bowstring, reveals her four-armed form and gives him a priceless ring and treasure _(actors: Caṇḍī, Kālketu, Phullarā)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/chandimangal-kalketu-and-srimanta.md) — (Mukundarāma, Caṇḍīmaṅgal, Ākheṭika khaṇḍa)
- **Caṇḍīmaṅgal dramatizes Maṅgalā Caṇḍī** — Mukundarāma's late-16th-c. Bengali maṅgal-kāvya recasts the fortune-goddess as the deity who compels worship from the hunter Kālketu and the merchant Dhanapati _(actors: Maṅgalā Caṇḍī, Kālketu, Dhanapati, Mukundarāma)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/prakriti-khanda-goddess-origins.md) — (Caṇḍīmaṅgal, c. 1590s)
- **Canonization as Tirumurai Book 12 (by c. 1313 CE)** — Umāpati Śivācārya's Cēkkiḻār Purāṇam records the composition legend, by which time the epic is fixed as the twelfth book of the Śaiva canon [scholarly] _(actors: Umāpati Śivācārya, Cēkkiḻār)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/periya-puranam-63-nayanmar-legend-map.md) — (Cēkkiḻār Purāṇam)
- **Cāntu's betrayal and the dying hero** — the wounded victor sends Cāntu to fetch his people, but the kinsman-traitor instead strips the dying Ārōmal of his gold ring and carries home a lying account _(actors: Ārōmal Cēkavar, Cāntu)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/vadakkan-pattukal-northern-ballads.md) — (Vaṭakkan Pāṭṭukaḷ, Ārōmal Cēkavar pāṭṭu)
- **Caturvarga-cintāmaṇi codified** — Hemādri, minister of the Yādavas of Devagiri, writes the largest of all nibandhas, whose Vrata-khaṇḍa catalogs hundreds of vows and festivals _(actors: Hemādri, Mahādeva Yādava, Rāmacandra Yādava)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/dharma-nibandhas-and-digests.md) — ([scholarly] c. 1260–1270 CE)
- **Cēkkiḻār composes the Periya Purāṇam (c. 1135 CE)** — the chief minister, given the grace-word "Ulagelām" by Śiva at Chidambaram per later legend, premieres the 4,286-verse epic in the Thousand-Pillared Hall under Kulōttuṅga II [scholarly] _(actors: Cēkkiḻār, Kulōttuṅga II, Śiva)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/periya-puranam-63-nayanmar-legend-map.md) — (Periya Purāṇam; Cēkkiḻār Purāṇam)
- **Ceṅkuṭṭuvaṉ consecrates the Pattini temple at Vañci** — The Chera king brings Himalayan stone and enshrines Kaṇṇaki as Pattini at Vañci — traditionally identified with Kodungallur — with Gajabāhu of Laṅkā attending (the disputed c. 2nd-century-CE synchronism) _(actors: Ceṅkuṭṭuvaṉ, Kaṇṇaki/Pattini, Gajabāhu)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/bhadrakali-kerala-bhagavati.md) — (Cilappatikāram, Vañcikkāṇṭam) [disputed]
- **Ceṅkuṭṭuvaṉ's Himalayan stone expedition** — the Chera king marches north, defeats the Āryan kings Kanaka and Vijaya, and has them carry back on their heads the Himalayan stone, Gaṅgā-bathed, for Pattini's image _(actors: Ceṅkuṭṭuvaṉ, Kanaka, Vijaya)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/silappatikaram-kannagi-pattini.md) — (Cilappatikāram, Kālkōṭ kātai)
- **Chaitanya relishes the Gīta-Govinda at Purī (c. 1510–1534 CE)** — Caitanya Mahāprabhu tastes Jayadeva's songs day and night with Svarūpa Dāmodara and Rāmānanda Rāya, making the poem the emotional textbook of Gauḍīya Rādhā-bhāva _(actors: Caitanya Mahāprabhu, Svarūpa Dāmodara, Rāmānanda Rāya)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/jayadeva-and-the-gita-govinda.md) — (Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Madhya 2.77)
- **Charter of the Khudurukuṇī Oṣā** — Because Maṅgaḷā accepted a wronged girl's broken rice and set her world right, unmarried girls of coastal Odisha keep the goddess's oṣā on the Sundays of Bhādraba, offering khuda and reciting the Tāpoi kathā for their brothers' welfare _(actors: Maṅgaḷā, Tāpoi, the girls of coastal Odisha)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/tapoi-khudurukuni-osha.md) — (Khudurukuṇī Oṣā observance, Odia folk tradition)
- **Charter of the tulasī-exclusion in Gaṇeśa-pūjā** — The curse-exchange becomes the standing ritual rule barring tulasī leaves from Gaṇeśa worship (dūrvā grass offered instead), with a contested folk exception on Gaṇeśa Caturthī _(actors: Gaṇeśa, Tulasī/tulasī plant, devotees)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/tulasi-ganesha-curse.md) — (Brahmavaivarta Purāṇa, Gaṇapati-khaṇḍa; ritual digests)
- **Chemmarathi's curse and self-immolation** — hearing of his death Chemmarathi curses in grief and throws herself onto her husband's funeral pyre _(actors: Chemmarathi, Māndappan)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/kathivanur-veeran.md) — (Kathivanur Veeran thottam-pāṭṭu)
- **Chempakasseri absorbed by Travancore (c. 1746 CE)** — the Ambalappuzha principality that raised and endowed the temple is annexed by Marthanda Varma's expanding Travancore _(actors: Marthanda Varma, Chempakasseri house)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/ambalappuzha-krishna-palpayasam.md) — (documented history) [scholarly]
- **Chempakasseri Krishna enshrined at Ambalappuzha** — the Chempakasseri king Devanarayanan Thampuran installs Krishna as Parthasarathi, whip and conch in hand, as the kingdom's tutelary deity _(actors: Devanarayanan Thampuran, Krishna-Parthasarathi)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/ambalappuzha-krishna-palpayasam.md) — (Ambalappuzha temple tradition; [scholarly] 15th–17th c. CE)
- **Cīrāḷaṉ runs in alive** — at his mother's call the sacrificed boy comes running home whole and living, and the Bhairava and the curry both vanish _(actors: Cīrāḷaṉ, Veṇkāṭṭu Nangai)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/siruthondar-nayanar.md) — (Periya Purāṇam, Ciṟuttoṇṭa Purāṇam)
- **Ciṟuttoṇṭar at Vātāpi (642 CE)** — general Paranjōti takes Vātāpi for the Pallavas [scholarly], then retires to the sainthood crowned by the child-curry ordeal _(actors: Ciṟuttoṇṭar/Paranjōti, Narasiṃhavarman I, Śiva as Bhairava, Cīrāḷaṉ)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/periya-puranam-63-nayanmar-legend-map.md) — (Periya Purāṇam, Ciṟuttoṇṭa Purāṇam)
- **Ciṟuttoṇṭar named in the Tiruttoṇṭattokai** — Sundarar honours the child-curry devotee in verse 6 of his roll-call hymn, the seed-list from which the 63-Nāyaṉār canon was later fixed _(actors: Cuntarar / Sundarar, Ciṟuttoṇṭar)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/siruthondar-nayanar.md) — (Cuntarar, Tiruttoṇṭattokai v. 6)
- **Ciṟuttoṇṭar's feeding-vow** — the retired general vows never to eat without first feeding a devotee of Śiva, keeping the vow with his wife Veṇkāṭṭu Nangai, son Cīrāḷaṉ, and servant at Tirucceṅkāṭṭaṅkuḍi _(actors: Ciṟuttoṇṭar, Veṇkāṭṭu Nangai, Cīrāḷaṉ)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/siruthondar-nayanar.md) — (Periya Purāṇam, Ciṟuttoṇṭa Purāṇam)
- **Cōḻa foundation of the shrine** — the stone temple over the sacred well is traditionally endowed by the Cōḻa emperor Kulottuṅga Cōḻa I in the late 11th century CE _(actors: Kulottuṅga Cōḻa I)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/kanipakam-varasiddhi-vinayaka.md) — (temple tradition) [scholarly] c. 1070–1120 CE
- **Colonial suppression of Charak hook-swinging (1865)** — The Bengal government prohibits the hook-swinging ordeal of the Gajan–Charak Puja; the rite goes underground and persists only patchily _(actors: British Bengal government, Charak sannyasis/bhoktas)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/regional-temple-festivals.md) — (Wikipedia, *Charak Puja*; Banglapedia)
- **Compilation of the Garuḍa Purāṇa's Preta-khaṇḍa** — the death-rite section accretes onto the layered first-millennium-CE Purāṇa (core estimates 4th–11th c., developed form by c. 900–1000 CE) [scholarly] _(actors: anonymous compilers, Vyāsa (traditional))_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/babhruvahana-and-the-preta.md) — (Garuḍa Purāṇa, Preta-khaṇḍa; academic dating)
- **Composition and stratification of the Padma Purāṇa** — [scholarly] the text compiles across roughly the 4th–15th c. CE (core c. 750–1000 CE), diverging into a Bengal 5-khaṇḍa and a printed 6-khaṇḍa recension with the Kriyāyogasāra-khaṇḍa as a late seventh supplement _(actors: anonymous compilers, H. H. Wilson (analyst))_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/puranas/padma-purana-khanda-map.md) — (Padma Purāṇa, recension history)
- **Composition of the Acchodā Pitṛ-kalpa accounts** — The Harivaṃśa (c. 1st–3rd c. CE onward) and the Matsya Purāṇa's early core (c. 200–500 CE) commit the Acchodā–Satyavatī rebirth frame to writing as śrāddha doctrine [scholarly] _(actors: Purāṇic redactors)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/acchoda-satyavati-rebirth.md) — (Matsya Purāṇa, 14; Harivaṃśa, Pitṛ-kalpa)
- **Composition of the Asya Vamasya riddle hymn (RV 1.164)** — The 52-verse brahmodya attributed to Dirghatamas, containing the two-birds verse (1.164.20) and 'ekam sad vipra bahudha vadanti' (1.164.46), is composed in the later Rigvedic period, c. 1200-1000 BCE [scholarly] _(actors: Dirghatamas (seer-family))_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/dirghatamas.md) — (Rigveda 1.164; Sarvanukramani)
- **Composition of the Bhāgavata Purāṇa (Skandha 11)** — [scholarly] dating places the Purāṇa, with its Nimi saṃvāda and southern river-praise, c. 8th–10th century CE, likely in the Tamil south _(actors: Bhāgavata redactors)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/nimi-nava-yogendra-samvada.md) — (Bhāgavata Purāṇa, 11.2–5)
- **Composition of the Bhāgavata-Māhātmya (c. 16th–18th c. CE) [scholarly]** — The Māhātmya enters the Padma Purāṇa's Uttara-khaṇḍa as a late addition, dated by scholars (Hawley) to early-modern North India, its Bhakti-journey verse becoming the charter of the 'bhakti movement' idea and of the modern saptāha _(actors: anonymous compilers, saptāha tradition)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/bhagavata-mahatmya-gokarna-dhundhukari.md) — (Padma Purāṇa, Uttara-khaṇḍa 193–198; Hawley, A Storm of Songs)
- **Composition of the Brahmavaivarta Purāṇa's Tulasī–Gaṇeśa saṃvāda** — The extant Bengal recension containing the Gaṇapati-khaṇḍa dialogue is dated c. 15th–16th century CE, with possible earlier strata [scholarly] _(actors: Brahmavaivarta Purāṇa compilers)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/tulasi-ganesha-curse.md) — (Brahmavaivarta Purāṇa, Gaṇapati-khaṇḍa)
- **Composition of the Cilappatikāram** — Iḷaṅkō Aṭikaḷ's epic of Kaṇṇaki, charter of the Pattini/Bhagavati assimilation, is composed — traditionally c. 100–250 CE, by modern scholarly consensus c. 5th century CE _(actors: Iḷaṅkō Aṭikaḷ)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/bhadrakali-kerala-bhagavati.md) — (Cilappatikāram; Zvelebil; Mahadevan) [scholarly]
- **Composition of the Cūḍālā tale (Mokṣopāya, Kashmir, c. 950 CE)** — The Cūḍālā-ākhyāna is composed within the Kashmiri Mokṣopāya, later Vedānticized into the vulgate Yoga-Vāsiṣṭha by c. the 14th century [scholarly] _(actors: anonymous Kashmiri author, later redactors)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/chudala-shikhidhvaja.md) — (Mokṣopāya / Yoga-Vāsiṣṭha VI.1, chs. ~77–110)
- **Composition of the Ekādaśī-māhātmya corpus** — the Padma Purāṇa's Uttara-khaṇḍa māhātmyas, the Kriyāyogasāra cycle, and the Nārada Purāṇa's Rukmāṅgada legend are judged post-1000 CE vrata literature of the medieval Vaiṣṇava fasting economy _(actors: Padma Purāṇa redactors)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/ekadashi-mahatmyas-catalog.md) — (Padma Purāṇa, Uttara-khaṇḍa; Kriyāyogasāra 22–23) [scholarly]
- **Composition of the Gambler's Lament** — a gambler's confession of ruin by the vibhidaka-nut dice, ending in Savitr's counsel 'play not with dice; plough your field,' composed in the late Mandala 10 stratum c. 1200–1000 BCE [scholarly] _(actors: the gambler, Savitr, Kavasha Ailusha)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/vedas/rigveda-samvada-and-secular-hymns.md) — (Rigveda 10.34)
- **Composition of the Gīta-Govinda (c. 1170–1200 CE)** — Jayadeva composes the 12-sarga / 24-aṣṭapadī Sanskrit lyric, opening with the Daśāvatāra-stotra, in the milieu of Lakṣmaṇasena's Bengal court [scholarly] _(actors: Jayadeva, Padmāvatī, Lakṣmaṇasena)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/jayadeva-and-the-gita-govinda.md) — (Gīta-Govinda, sarga 1)
- **Composition of the Kālikā Purāṇa in Kāmarūpa** — the fullest telling of the Lauhitya birth-legend is composed in Assam c. 10th–11th century CE [scholarly] _(actors: Kāmarūpa redactors)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/lauhitya-brahmaputra-origin.md) — (Kālikā Purāṇa, 82–85)
- **Composition of the māsa-māhātmya strata** — the month-glorification layers of the Padma and Skanda Purāṇas take shape as late-medieval vrata literature, the Puruṣottama-māhātmya latest of all [scholarly] _(actors: anonymous purāṇic compilers)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/masa-mahatmyas-kartika-magha-vaishakha.md) — (Hazra, Purāṇic Records; Rocher, The Purāṇas)
- **Composition of the Mokṣopāya / Yoga-Vāsiṣṭha** — The anonymous Kashmiri Mokṣopāya is composed c. 950 CE and later Vedānticized into the vulgate Yoga-Vāsiṣṭha attributed to Vālmīki, its ~25 nested parables carrying the ajāta-vāda doctrine [scholarly] _(actors: anonymous Kashmiri author, later redactors)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/yoga-vasishtha-tales-catalog.md) — (Mokṣopāya studies; Yoga-Vāsiṣṭha vulgate)
- **Composition of the Nārada Bhakti Sūtra** — the 84 aphorisms on parā-prema, the eleven āsaktis and gauṇī vs parā bhakti crystallize, commonly dated c. 10th–12th c. CE, post-Bhāgavata [late text] [scholarly] _(actors: Nārada (attributed))_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/bhakti-sutras-narada-shandilya.md) — (Nārada Bhakti Sūtra, 1–84)
- **Composition of the Nīlamata Purāṇa** — Kashmir's charter text — origin myth, nāga-spring roster, and festival calendar — is composed in the valley c. 6th–8th century CE [scholarly] _(actors: anonymous Kashmiri redactors)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/puranas/nilamata-purana.md) — (Bühler 1877; Ghai, The Nīlamata Purāṇa)
- **Composition of the Nyāya-sudhā** — Jayatīrtha writes the "Nectar of Logic" on Madhva's Anuvyākhyāna, the definitive post-founder work of Dvaita Vedānta, before his death c. 1388 CE [scholarly] _(actors: Jayatīrtha, Madhva)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/raghavendra-tirtha-and-the-dvaita-line.md) — (Sharma, History of the Dvaita School)
- **Composition of the Padma Uttara-khaṇḍa tīrtha-māhātmyas [scholarly]** — the Puṇḍarīka chapters belong to the Uttara-khaṇḍa's late māhātmya strata of a Purāṇa compiled c. 4th–15th c. CE (core layers c. 750–1000 CE) _(actors: Padma Purāṇa redactors)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/pundarika-of-pushkara.md) — (Padma Purana, Uttara-khanda 218–219; Rocher 1986)
- **Composition of the Puruṣa Sūkta** — The hymn is composed within the late stratum of Ṛgveda maṇḍala 10, c. 1200–1000 BCE [scholarly] _(actors: ṛṣi Nārāyaṇa (traditional attribution), Vedic poets)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/stotras/purusha-sukta.md) — (Ṛgveda, 10.90)
- **Composition of the Śāṇḍilya Bhakti Sūtra** — ~100 aphorisms opening "athāto bhakti-jijñāsā" argue bhakti (not jñāna) liberates; dating spans early centuries CE to early 2nd millennium [scholarly] [disputed] _(actors: Śāṇḍilya (attributed))_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/bhakti-sutras-narada-shandilya.md) — (Śāṇḍilya Bhakti Sūtra, 1.1)
- **Composition of the Sudarśana–Śaśikalā charter narrative** — the Devī Bhāgavata's compilers (c. 9th–14th century CE [scholarly]) compose Skandha III's original story-plus-vidhi unit to legitimize royal Navarātri and Durgā-pūjā at Benares and Ayodhyā _(actors: Devī Bhāgavata compilers)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/sudarshana-shashikala-navaratri.md) — (Devī Bhāgavata Purāṇa, Skandha III; Brown 1990; Pintchman 1994)
- **Composition of the Vālmīki Rāmāyaṇa** — the core books (Kāṇḍas 2–6) are composed c. 7th–4th c. BCE with the Bāla and Uttara Kāṇḍas as later strata, per the critical-edition consensus [scholarly] _(actors: Vālmīki tradition)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/characters/rama.md) — (Baroda Critical Edition scholarship)
- **Composition of the vānara origin-strata** — the Bāla- and Uttara-kāṇḍas carrying both vānara origin accounts are dated as later additions to the c. 7th–4th c. BCE core epic [scholarly] _(actors: Vālmīki tradition)_ → [details](../08-beings-and-bestiary/vanaras-and-rikshas.md) — (Goldman, The Rāmāyaṇa of Vālmīki)
- **Composition of Viṣṇu Purāṇa Aṃśa III's closing orthodoxy narrative** — the Śatadhanu–Śaibyā parable and its anti-pāṣaṇḍa rules are dated c. 400 BCE–450 CE [scholarly], read as brahmanical boundary polemic against Buddhist/Jain competition _(actors: Purāṇic compilers)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/shatadhanu-shaibya.md) — (Viṣṇu Purāṇa, III.18)
- **Condemned to the stake** — seized on the Brahmins' complaint, Kāttavarāyaṉ is sentenced to death by impalement, the caste order's verdict on a boundary it cannot forgive _(actors: Kāttavarāyaṉ, the Brahmins)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/kattavarayan-katai.md) — (Kaṭṭavarāyacuvāmi Kataippāṭal) [oral tradition]
- **Consecration of Pattini at Vañci with Gajabāhu attending** — Ceṅkuṭṭuvaṉ enshrines Kaṇṇaki as Pattini at Vañci, with "Kayavāku of sea-girt Laṅkā" among the dignitaries — the disputed Gajabāhu synchronism anchoring the events to c. 2nd c. CE _(actors: Ceṅkuṭṭuvaṉ, Kaṇṇaki/Pattini, Gajabāhu I)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/silappatikaram-kannagi-pattini.md) — (Cilappatikāram, Varantaru kātai) [disputed]
- **Contest with Sambandāṇḍāṉ** — at King Prabhuḍa Deva Rāya's court a jealous Śākta rival challenges Aruṇagiri to a contest of deities, and Murugan manifests where the Goddess does not _(actors: Aruṇagirinātar, Sambandāṇḍāṉ, Murugan, Prabhuḍa Deva Rāya)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/arunagirinathar-tiruppugazh.md) — (hagiographic tradition)
- **Cradle-birth of Ramdev at Undu-Kashmir** — Ramdev appears miraculously in the cradle in Ajmal's palace, hailed as Krishna's Kali-yuga avatara, traditionally Bhadrapada shukla 2, V.S. 1409 (c. 1352 CE) _(actors: Ramdev, Ajmal, Mainade)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/ramdev-pir.md) — (Ramdev hagiographic tradition) [folk] [disputed]
- **Dang Hyang Nirartha arrives in Bali (c. 1537) and attains mokṣa at Uluwatu** — the last great Javanese brahmin reforms the pedanda priesthood, institutes the padmasana shrine and consecrates the sea-temple chain [traditional] _(actors: Dang Hyang Nirartha, Dalem Baturenggong)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/balinese-hinduism-agama-hindu-dharma.md) — (Babad Dwijendra)
- **Dāsiā Baurī sends a coconut** — barred from the sanctum as an untouchable, the Baurī devotee hands his coconut to a mocking Brāhmaṇ pilgrim to offer, insisting the Lord will take it in his own hand _(actors: Dāsiā Baurī, a Brāhmaṇ pilgrim)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/characters/jagannatha-bhakta-legends.md) — (Odia bhakta-caritra) [oral tradition]
- **Dāyabhāga composed** — Jīmūtavāhana of Bengal writes the rival inheritance digest holding a son gains no right by birth and heirship follows spiritual benefit (piṇḍa-offering), founding the Bengal school _(actors: Jīmūtavāhana)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/dharma-nibandhas-and-digests.md) — ([scholarly] c. 1090–1130 CE)
- **Death against the Kodagu raiders** — fighting valorously but surrounded, Māndappan is slain and his fingers severed, and he gropes the battlefield for them as he dies _(actors: Māndappan, Kodava raiders)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/kathivanur-veeran.md) — (Kathivanur Veeran thottam-pāṭṭu)
- **Death fighting the cattle-rustlers** — when raiders drive off the countryside's herds, Muttuppaṭṭaṉ pursues them, fights to recover the cattle and is cut down — the violent, premature death that makes a god _(actors: Muttuppaṭṭaṉ, the cattle-rustlers)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/muttuppattan-katai.md) — (Muttuppaṭṭaṉ Katai; Blackburn, Singing of Birth and Death)
- **Death of Maṇavāḷa Māmunigaḷ** — the Teṅkalai consolidator, revered as Rāmānuja reborn, passes at Śrīraṅgam in 1443 [traditional], his aṣṭa-dig-gaja disciples carrying the line onward _(actors: Maṇavāḷa Māmunigaḷ, Ponnaḍikkāl Jīyar)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/vedanta-desika-and-later-srivaishnava-acharyas.md) — (Wikipedia, Manavala Mamunigal)
- **Death of Tulsīdās at Asī Ghāṭ** — the poet dies on the Gaṅgā's bank at Vārāṇasī, V.S. 1680 (1623 CE), per the tradition-couplet "saṃvat solaha sau asī...", after composing the Hanumān Bāhuka for his ailing arm _(actors: Tulsīdās)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/tulsidas.md) — (tradition-couplet [traditional]; Wikipedia, Tulsidas)
- **Deification of Muttuppaṭṭaṉ as guardian god** — the martyred brahmin-cobbler becomes a kāval deyvam of the Tirunelveli country, honored at koṭai festivals with bow-song and possession, devotees offering leather sandals at his Tāmraparṇī-country shrine _(actors: Muttuppaṭṭaṉ, Bommakkā, Timmakkā, bow-song singers, devotees)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/muttuppattan-katai.md) — (vil pāṭṭu cult tradition; Muttuppaṭṭaṉ shrine tradition, Tirunelveli)
- **Deification of the jāgar devtās (Katyūrī–Chand era)** — the wronged dead of Kumaon — prince Bholānāth, the jogī-lovers Gaṅgānāth and Bhānā, the murdered herdsman Kalbiśṭ — are pacified and enshrined as possession deities _(actors: Bholānāth, Gaṅgānāth, Bhānā, Kalbiśṭ)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/nanda-devi-and-uttarakhand-deities.md) — (Kumaoni jāgar ballads [folk] [oral tradition])
- **Deification of the martyr-couple** — the fallen hero and self-burning wife are raised to godhood as the theyyam deities Kathivanur Veeran and Chemmarathi _(actors: Kathivanur Veeran, Chemmarathi)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/kathivanur-veeran.md) — (K.K.N. Kurup, The Cult of Teyyam)
- **Deśika escapes the Śrīraṅgam massacre with the Śrutaprakāśikā** — hiding among the slain, he saves Sudarśana Sūri's Śrī Bhāṣya sub-commentary and the author's two sons, c. 1327 [traditional] _(actors: Vedānta Deśika, Sudarśana Sūri)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/vedanta-desika-and-later-srivaishnava-acharyas.md) — (Wikipedia, Vedanta Desika)
- **Devī-Bhāgavata Book IX and the Brahmavaivarta Prakṛti-khaṇḍa composed** — the late Śākta corpus issuing Prakṛti-pedigrees to the Bengal folk goddesses; DBP c. 9th–14th c. CE, extant Brahmavaivarta a 15th–16th-c. Bengal recomposition [scholarly] _(actors: anonymous Bengal-region redactors)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/gramadevata-shashthi-cross-links.md) — (Devī-Bhāgavata, IX; Brahmavaivarta, Prakṛti-khaṇḍa)
- **Dhanapati reconciled and Caṇḍī-pūjā established among merchants** — The stubborn Śaiva merchant accepts the goddess on seeing her one with Śiva, and her worship is planted in the bāṇik community _(actors: Dhanapati, Caṇḍī, Śiva, Khullanā, Śrīmanta)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/chandimangal-kalketu-and-srimanta.md) — (Mukundarāma, Caṇḍīmaṅgal, Bāṇik khaṇḍa)
- **Dhanapati sees the Kamale-Kāminī and is imprisoned in Ceylon** — After kicking over Caṇḍī's ghaṭ, the Śaiva merchant beholds the lotus-woman swallowing an elephant at Kālīdaha, cannot show the withheld vision to the Siṁhala king, and is jailed as a liar _(actors: Dhanapati, Caṇḍī (Kamale-Kāminī), the Siṁhala king)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/chandimangal-kalketu-and-srimanta.md) — (Mukundarāma, Caṇḍīmaṅgal, Bāṇik khaṇḍa)
- **Dharmasindhu completed** — Kāśīnātha Upādhyāya writes the practical household ritual manual of the late Peśvā Deccan, still the working authority behind everyday domestic observance _(actors: Kāśīnātha Upādhyāya)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/dharma-nibandhas-and-digests.md) — (Dharmasindhu, 1790–91 CE)
- **Dinā and Bhadrī martyred defending the Musahar hamlets** — The Musahar brother-heroes of the Kosi belt, champions of their community's fields and honour against demons/oppressors (variant-dependent), die violently in their final battle rather than submit _(actors: Dinā, Bhadrī, Musahar community)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/maithili-oral-epics-salhesh-dina-bhadri.md) — (Dinā-Bhadrī gāthā) [oral tradition]
- **Dinā-Bhadrī deified as ancestral gods of the Musahars** — After their martyrdom the brothers are worshipped at thān shrines in Musahar tolas of the Kosi country with night-long bhagait possession-singing and offerings of pigs and liquor, outside Brahmin priesthood _(actors: Dinā, Bhadrī, bhagait singers, Musahar community)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/maithili-oral-epics-salhesh-dina-bhadri.md) — (Dinā-Bhadrī cult, Kosi belt) [folk]
- **Dvija Mādhava's Caṇḍīmaṅgal composed** — The earlier dated recension of the Caṇḍīmaṅgal (Sāradāmaṅgal), internally dated Śaka 1501 = 1579 CE [scholarly] _(actors: Dvija Mādhava)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/chandimangal-kalketu-and-srimanta.md) — (Dvija Mādhava, Caṇḍīmaṅgal)
- **E. B. Cowell translates the Śāṇḍilya sūtra (1878)** — sūtra-plus-Svapneśvara published in English at Calcutta, opening the text to Western scholarship [scholarly] _(actors: E. B. Cowell, Svapneśvara)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/bhakti-sutras-narada-shandilya.md) — (Cowell, The Aphorisms of Śāṇḍilya, 1878)
- **Earliest claimed grand Durgā Pūjā at Tāherpur (c. 1580 CE)** — tradition credits zamindar Kaṁsanārāyaṇ of Tāherpur with the first Śāradīyā Durgā Pūjā in near-modern form, a claim rivaled and undocumentable _(actors: Kaṁsanārāyaṇ of Tāherpur)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/durga-puja-bengal.md) — (Bengali historiography) [scholarly][disputed]
- **Earliest historical attestation of the Vṛṣṇi cult** — Pāṇini's Vāsudeva-devotees, the Arthaśāstra's Vṛṣṇi saṅgha, the Besnagar Heliodorus pillar (c. 113 BCE) and the Mora well inscription document early Vāsudeva-Kṛṣṇa worship [scholarly] _(actors: Pāṇini, Kauṭilya, Heliodorus)_ → [details](../03-lineages-vamsha/yadava-vamsha.md) — (Aṣṭādhyāyī; Arthaśāstra; Besnagar & Mora inscriptions)
- **Earliest mention of Rādhā's name** — a single Prakrit verse of Hāla's anthology shows Kṛṣṇa blowing dust from Rādhā's face, the first surviving occurrence of her name (~1st–2nd c. CE) _(actors: Hāla, Rādhā, Kṛṣṇa)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/radha.md) — (Gāhā Sattasaī) [scholarly]
- **Earliest Saṃkarṣaṇa cult inscriptions [scholarly]** — the Ghosuṇḍī and Nānāghāṭ inscriptions (c. 2nd–1st century BCE) and the Mora well record invoke Saṃkarṣaṇa with Vāsudeva, attesting Balarāma's worship among the oldest documented Vaiṣṇava cults _(actors: Saṃkarṣaṇa-worshippers, Vṛṣṇi hero cult)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/characters/balarama.md) — (Ghosuṇḍī, Nānāghāṭ & Mora well inscriptions, epigraphy)
- **Edgerton's four recensions of the Vikramacarita** — the Siṃhāsana-dvātriṃśikā survives in four divergent Sanskrit recensions (Southern, Metrical, Brief, Jainistic), critically edited and translated by Franklin Edgerton as *Vikrama's Adventures* (HOS 26–27, 1926) _(actors: Franklin Edgerton)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/simhasana-dvatrimshika-vikramaditya-cycle.md) — (Edgerton, HOS 26–27) [scholarly]
- **Eka Dasa Rudra interrupted by the Gunung Agung eruption (1963); completed 1979** — the once-a-century purification of the eleven Rudras at Pura Besakih is shattered by the volcano's deadly eruption mid-ceremony, then performed anew a cycle later [scholarly] _(actors: pedanda priesthood, Pura Besakih, Gunung Agung)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/balinese-hinduism-agama-hindu-dharma.md) — (documented history)
- **Emergence of the Satya Pīr cult in Bengal** — the deity of the vrata circulates as the fakir Satya Pīr, worshipped by Hindus and Muslims alike with the śirṇi offering, traceable in Bengali literature from the 16th century _(actors: Satya Pīr, Bengali Hindu and Muslim devotees)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/satyanarayana-vrata-katha.md) — (Satya Pīr pāñcālīs; Stewart) [scholarly] [regional] [folk]
- **Emperor Harṣa writes Nāgānanda, Ratnāvalī and Priyadarśikā** — royal playwright of Kanauj (r. 606–647 CE) stages Jīmūtavāhana's self-sacrifice to Garuḍa with a Buddhist benediction [scholarly] _(actors: Harṣavardhana, Jīmūtavāhana, Garuḍa, Gaurī)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/classical-kavya-and-sanskrit-drama.md) — (Nāgānanda 1–5; Bāṇa, Harṣacarita)
- **Endowments deeded to Caṇḍeśvara** — in Pallava–Chola epigraphy temple lands are granted "to Caṇḍeśvara" as the temple's legal proprietor, translating his stewardship into property law (e.g. the Bṛhadīśvara, Tañjāvūr) _(actors: Caṇḍeśvara, Chola donors)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/characters/chandesvara-chandikeshvara.md) — ([scholarly] Chola epigraphy, c. 7th–11th c. CE)
- **English translations and modern afterlife** — Burton's retelling (1870), Tawney's translation (1880–84), Ryder's Twenty-two Goblins (1917), Penzer's Ocean of Story (1924–28), and Doordarshan's Vikram aur Betaal (1985) carry the cycle worldwide [scholarly] _(actors: Richard Burton, C. H. Tawney, A. W. Ryder, N. M. Penzer, Ramanand Sagar)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/vetala-panchavimshati-vikram-and-betal.md) — (Ocean of Story vols. VI–VII)
- **Enrolled in Sundarar's Tiruttoṇḍattokai** — Sundarar honours the labourer-saint as "Tirunāḷaippōvār" in his roll-call hymn, the seed-list from which the canonical 63 Nāyaṉārs were later fixed _(actors: Cuntarar / Sundarar, Nandanar)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/nandanar-tirunalaippovar.md) — (Cuntarar, Tiruttoṇḍattokai)
- **Fables of Bidpai printed in Europe** — John of Capua's Latin Directorium Humanae Vitae printed 1480 CE [scholarly], spawning the German, Spanish and English (North, 1570) Bidpai lines later credited by La Fontaine _(actors: John of Capua, Thomas North, La Fontaine)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/panchatantra-hitopadesha-and-fable-literature.md) — (Directorium Humanae Vitae, transmission history)
- **Fall of Majapahit and the Hindu exodus to Bali (c. 1478–1527)** — as Islamic Demak rises, Majapahit's priests, poets and nobles migrate east, making Bali the ark of Javano-Hindu civilization [scholarly] _(actors: Majapahit priests and nobility, Demak sultanate)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/balinese-hinduism-agama-hindu-dharma.md) — (babad chronicles)
- **Fall of the Videha monarchy** — The Arthaśāstra remembers Karāla the Vaideha as destroyed by lust; historically the Videha kingship gives way to the Vajji confederacy centred on Vaiśālī by the Buddha's time [scholarly] _(actors: Karāla the Vaideha, the Vajji confederacy)_ → [details](../03-lineages-vamsha/videha-mithila-line-janaka.md) — (Arthaśāstra 1.6)
- **Fed at night by the Lord's command** — moved by the poor man's grief, Jagannātha commands in a servitor's dream that the temple be reopened after hours and Bandhu Mohanty be fed mahāprasāda, the sleeping Lord's kitchen unstopped for one shut-out bhakta _(actors: Jagannātha, Bandhu Mohanty, the temple servitor)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/characters/jagannatha-bhakta-legends.md) — (Odia bhakta-caritra) [oral tradition] [folk]
- **First community bāro-yāri pūjā at Guptipara (1790 CE)** — twelve friends barred from a household pūjā pool subscriptions and hold their own, the first recorded community-subscription Durgā Pūjā _(actors: the twelve friends of Guptipara)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/durga-puja-bengal.md) — (Bengali festival historiography) [scholarly]
- **First jauhar of Chittor (1303 CE)** — At ʿAlāʾ-ud-dīn Khaljī's conquest of Chittorgarh the fort's women immolate collectively; later legend centers the fire on queen Padminī via Jāyasī's Padmāvat [disputed] [late text] _(actors: ʿAlāʾ-ud-dīn Khaljī, Padminī, Ratansen)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/sati-jauhar-and-the-immolation-rites.md) — (Padmāvat of Jāyasī, 1540; Sultanate chronicles)
- **First print documentation of the Maithili hero-songs (1880s)** — George A. Grierson's Maithili collections are credited with the earliest recorded specimens of the region's folk songs, including Dinā-Bhadrī material, a century before sustained ethnography _(actors: George A. Grierson, Maithili singers)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/maithili-oral-epics-salhesh-dina-bhadri.md) — (Grierson, Maithili collections, 1880s) [scholarly]
- **First Rāmlīlā instituted at Vārāṇasī** — tradition credits Tulsīdās and his disciple Megha Bhagat with founding the Rāmlīlā, the open-air enactment of Rāma's story with the Mānas as recited script, ancestor of the Rāmnagar Rāmlīlā and of the form UNESCO honored (2005/2008) _(actors: Tulsīdās, Megha Bhagat)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/tulsidas.md) — (UNESCO ICH, Ramlila; Lutgendorf, The Life of a Text [traditional])
- **First sarbajanin (public) pūjā at Bagbazar (1910 CE)** — the Sanātan Dharmotsāhinī Sabhā holds the first fully public all-comers Durgā Pūjā in Calcutta, the form that became dominant and fused with nationalist Bhārat-Mātā symbolism _(actors: Sanātan Dharmotsāhinī Sabhā, Bagbazar)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/durga-puja-bengal.md) — (Bengali festival historiography) [scholarly]
- **Gajabāhu brings the Pattini cult to Laṅkā** — late Sinhala chronicle tradition has Gajabāhu I import Pattini worship and her anklet-relic from the Tamil country, the foundation-legend of the Sinhala cult _(actors: Gajabāhu I, Pattini)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/silappatikaram-kannagi-pattini.md) — (Rājāvaliya; Obeyesekere) [late text] [disputed]
- **Gauḍīya deployment of 11.5.32 as Caitanya proof-text** — the 16th-century Caitanya school reads the saṅkīrtana verse as the Bhāgavata's prophecy of the golden hidden avatāra, canonized in the Caitanya-caritāmṛta _(actors: Caitanya Mahāprabhu, Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/nimi-nava-yogendra-samvada.md) — (Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Ādi 3)
- **Gaura-gaṇoddeśa-dīpikā identifies the Gosvāmīs as mañjarīs** — Kavi-karṇapūra equates Rūpa with Rūpa-mañjarī, canonizing mañjarī-bhāva as the Gauḍīya siddha identity (1576 CE [late text]) _(actors: Kavi-karṇapūra, Rūpa Gosvāmī, Rādhā)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/bhakti-rasa-and-the-bhavas.md) — (Gaura-gaṇoddeśa-dīpikā)
- **Gīta-Govinda canonizes Rādhā** — Jayadeva's twelve-canto lyric (12th c. CE) makes Rādhā the central heroine of Kṛṣṇa devotion, with Kṛṣṇa begging her foot upon his head, and enters nightly liturgy at Jagannātha Purī _(actors: Jayadeva, Rādhā, Kṛṣṇa)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/radha.md) — (Gīta-Govinda, canto 10) [scholarly]
- **Golu Devtā's life and deification (Katyūrī era)** — the prince born to King Jhāl Rāi and Kālinkā, cast into the river by jealous co-queens, exposes the fraud by the wooden-horse riddle, rules justly, and after death becomes Kumaon's god of justice _(actors: Golu Devtā, Jhāl Rāi, Kālinkā, the seven queens)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/nanda-devi-and-uttarakhand-deities.md) — (Kumaoni Golu jāgar ballad [folk] [oral tradition])
- **Gōpālakr̥ṣṇa Bhāratī's Nandaṉ Carittiram (1861)** — the Tamil composer recasts the Nāyaṉār's life as a Carnatic musical drama that outstrips its scriptural source and makes Nandanar the flashpoint of the caste-and-bhakti debate _(actors: Gōpālakr̥ṣṇa Bhāratī, Nandanar, the landlord Vēdiyar)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/nandanar-tirunalaippovar.md) — (Gōpālakr̥ṣṇa Bhāratī, Nandaṉ Carittira Kīrttaṉai, 1861)
- **Gopaṇa restores Raṅganātha to Śrīraṅgam** — the Vijayanagara commander returns the exiled image c. 1371 and the centenarian Deśika composes praise-verses preserved in epigraphy [scholarly] _(actors: Gopaṇa of Gingee, Vedānta Deśika, Raṅganātha)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/vedanta-desika-and-later-srivaishnava-acharyas.md) — (Wikipedia, Ranganathaswamy Temple Srirangam)
- **Gopīcandra's refusal** — the young enthroned king rejects his mother's demand, disbelieving the prophecy and unable to abandon his queens Aduna and Paduna _(actors: Gopīcandra, Maynāmatī, Aduna, Paduna)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/gopichandra-maynamati-nath-epic.md) — (Gopīcandrer Gān) [folk]
- **Gorakhnāth traps the nāgas and Machhindranāth is brought from Kāmarūpa** — the insulted yogī sits on the rain-serpents causing a twelve-year drought until his guru Matsyendranātha is fetched from Assam and enshrined at Bungamati (legend set under the Licchavi king Narendradeva, trad. 7th c. CE) _(actors: Gorakhnāth, Machhindranāth/Matsyendranātha, Narendradeva, Bandhudatta Ācārya)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/nepal-kumari-taleju-machhindranath.md) — (Kathmandu Valley legend; Newar chronicle tradition) [folk]
- **Govinda Dīkṣita builds the tank maṇḍapas (early 17th c.)** — the Thanjavur Nāyak minister renovates the Mahāmaham tank and raises its sixteen maṇḍapas, fixing the festival's built stage _(actors: Govinda Dīkṣita, the Thanjavur Nāyaks)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/kumbakonam-adi-kumbheshwara-mahamaham.md) — (temple/inscriptional record) [scholarly]
- **Grierson recovers the Rangpur song** — G. A. Grierson publishes a Rangpur singer's Māṇik Chandra Rājār Gān in 1878, the foundational scholarly text later expanded by D. C. Sen's Gopīcandrer Gān (c. 1922–24) _(actors: G. A. Grierson, Dineśacandra Sen)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/gopichandra-maynamati-nath-epic.md) — (Grierson, JASB 1878; D. C. Sen) [scholarly]
- **Guṇāḍhya composes the Bṛhatkathā in Paiśācī** — bound by his lost grammar-wager to renounce Sanskrit, Prakrit and his own tongue, Guṇāḍhya hears the tale from Kāṇabhūti in the Vindhya forest and writes 700,000 ślokas in his own blood over seven years _(actors: Guṇāḍhya, Kāṇabhūti, Śarvavarman)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/brihatkatha-kathasaritsagara-and-vikramaditya-cycle.md) — (Kathāsaritsāgara, I.6–8)
- **Guruvayur idol sheltered from Tipu (1789 CE)** — as Tipu Sultan's forces raid Malabar, the Guruvayur processional image is evacuated south and given refuge at Ambalappuzha until worship is restored after 1792 _(actors: Tipu Sultan, Guruvayur priests, Ambalappuzha temple)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/ambalappuzha-krishna-palpayasam.md) — (Guruvayur temple chronicles) [scholarly]
- **Guruvāyūr Kesavan dies in praṇāma on Ekādaśī (2 December 1976 CE)** — the Gajarājan, gifted by the Nilambur kōvilakam, fasts, faces the sanctum, raises his trunk in salute and collapses on Guruvāyūr Ekādaśī day _(actors: Guruvāyūr Kesavan, Guruvayur Devaswom)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/guruvayur-krishna-kshetra.md) — (Guruvāyūr Devaswom records) [scholarly]
- **Guruvāyūr Satyāgraha (1931–32 CE) and temple entry (June 1947)** — K. Kelappan leads the landmark temple-entry campaign with A. K. Gopalan and P. Krishna Pillai, and entry for all Hindus follows in June 1947 _(actors: K. Kelappan, A. K. Gopalan, P. Krishna Pillai, Gandhi)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/guruvayur-krishna-kshetra.md) — (Documented history) [scholarly]
- **Haibatrāvbābā organizes the pālkhī system (late 18th–early 19th c.)** — the Scindia courtier Haibatrāvbābā Ārphaḷkar reorganizes the Jñāneśvar pālkhī from Āḷandī with its diṇḍī register, horses and staged halts, creating the modern Wārī order _(actors: Haibatrāvbābā Ārphaḷkar, Jñāneśvar pālkhī)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/pandharpur-vitthala-wari.md) — (Deleury; Vārkarī tradition) [scholarly] [disputed]
- **Hanumān darśana at Vārāṇasī and Citrakūṭ** — a grateful preta points Tulsī to Hanumān disguised as a leper at his kathā; Hanumān then grants him the sight of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa at Citrakūṭ's ghāṭ, sealed by the parrot-sung tilaka dohā _(actors: Tulsīdās, Hanumān, Rāma, Lakṣmaṇa)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/tulsidas.md) — (Mūla Gosāīṃ Carita [oral tradition])
- **Harbhuji Sankhla blesses Rao Jodha** — the warrior-turned-Nath-disciple of Bhundel, one of the five pirs, by tradition blesses Rao Jodha's recovery of Mandore (mid-15th c. synchronism) before Jodhpur's founding [folk] [disputed] _(actors: Harbhuji Sankhla, Rao Jodha, Balinath)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/tejaji.md) — (Bhundel shrine tradition)
- **Harṣa's court-comedy sequels** — the emperor-playwright Harṣa sets Ratnāvalī and Priyadarśikā in Udayana's Kauśāmbī, adding a new beloved with Vāsavadattā already installed as senior queen _(actors: Harṣa, Udayana, Vāsavadattā, Sāgarikā)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/udayana-vasavadatta.md) — (Harṣa, Ratnāvalī & Priyadarśikā)
- **Harṣola copper-plates name the Nāgaras (949 CE)** — Rāṣṭrakūṭa grants record Nāgara Brāhmaṇas 'come from Ānandapura,' proving the community established centuries before the charter-myth's fullest form _(actors: the Harṣola donors, Nāgara Brāhmaṇas)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/vadnagar-hatakeshvara-kshetra.md) — (Harṣola copper-plates, 949 CE)
- **Havelī sevā and the pan-North-Indian song** — The Madhurāṣṭakam becomes a staple of Puṣṭimārga *aṣṭa-yāma sevā* and spreads across the Braj Kṛṣṇa-bhakti world as a beloved sung stotra _(actors: Puṣṭimārga havelīs, Braj devotees)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/stotras/madhurashtakam.md) — (living recitation tradition)
- **Head of the sāḍe-tīn circuit** — Tuljapur is reckoned the first of the three-and-a-half Śakti Pīṭhas of Maharashtra with Kolhapur, Māhūr and Saptaśṛṅgī, a living regional pilgrimage circuit _(actors: the pilgrims of the Marathi country)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/tuljapur-tulja-bhavani.md) — (Wikipedia, Three and a half Shakti Peethas) [regional] [folk]
- **Hindu Succession Act absorbs the schools** — the Mitākṣarā–Dāyabhāga divide that governed pre-modern Hindu inheritance is largely superseded by statute, with the coparcenary opened to daughters only in 2005 _(actors: Indian Parliament)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/dharma-nibandhas-and-digests.md) — ([scholarly] 1956; amended 2005)
- **Hita Harivaṃśa founds the Rādhā-vallabha Sampradāya** — the 16th-c. saint (trad. 1502–1552), initiated by Rādhā in a dream, establishes the order worshipping Rādhā as supreme with Kṛṣṇa as her servant, singing the Hita-caurāsī _(actors: Hita Harivaṃśa, Rādhā)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/radha.md) — (Rādhāvallabhī tradition; Hita-caurāsī) [late text]
- **Hitopadeśa attested** — Nārāyaṇa's four-book Bengal reworking of the Panchatantra (with Kāmandaki's Nītisāra), composed c. 800–1373 CE with its earliest manuscript dated 1373 CE [scholarly] _(actors: Nārāyaṇa, Dhavala Candra, Sudarśana of Pāṭaliputra)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/panchatantra-hitopadesha-and-fable-literature.md) — (Hitopadeśa, preamble)
- **Imperial imprisonment and the monkey siege** — summoned to Delhi and jailed for refusing to perform a miracle, Tulsī is freed when hordes of monkeys ransack the court; a popular telling has him compose the Hanumān Cālīsā's forty verses in forty days of this imprisonment _(actors: Tulsīdās, the emperor (Akbar/Jahāngīr in variant tellings), Hanumān)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/tulsidas.md) — (popular hagiography [oral tradition] [disputed])
- **Independent recensions by Śivadāsa and Jambhaladatta** — Two further Sanskrit versions (c. 11th–14th c. CE) recast the cycle, Śivadāsa naming the hero Vikramāditya and Jambhaladatta substituting tales of his own [scholarly] _(actors: Śivadāsa, Jambhaladatta)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/vetala-panchavimshati-vikram-and-betal.md) — (Vetālapañcaviṃśatikā; Emeneau ed.)
- **Initiation by Narharidās at Sūkarakṣetra** — the orphaned Rāmbolā is adopted and initiated by the Rāmānandī ascetic Narharidās, who recites the Rāma-story to him at Sūkarakheta (Soron) — the one episode Tulsī himself corroborates _(actors: Tulsīdās, Narharidās)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/tulsidas.md) — (Rāmcaritmānas, Bāl-kāṇḍa; Mūla Gosāīṃ Carita)
- **Initiation by the sweeper-siddha** — convinced at last, Gopīcandra renounces the throne and takes Nāth initiation from the low-caste Hāḍi-siddha, the king's pride broken to receive a scavenger as guru _(actors: Gopīcandra, Hāḍipā)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/gopichandra-maynamati-nath-epic.md) — (Gopīcandrer Gān; Nāth hagiography) [folk]
- **Jābāla Upaniṣad codifies immediate renunciation** — the earliest major Sannyāsa Upaniṣad (last centuries BCE–early CE [scholarly]) rules that one may go forth the very day detachment dawns, and scripts the praiṣa formula _(actors: Jābāla Upaniṣad compilers, Yājñavalkya as dialogue voice)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/sannyasa-and-the-renunciate-tradition.md) — (Jābāla Upaniṣad, 4)
- **Jagannātha Dāsa's Odia Bhāgabata and the bhāgabata-tuṅgī** — the navākṣarī-metre Bhāgavata becomes Odisha's household scripture, read nightly in village reading-houses, 16th century CE [scholarly] _(actors: Jagannātha Dāsa, Caitanya)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/odia-literature-sarala-and-panchasakha.md) — (Odia Bhāgabata; Odia social-religious history)
- **Jagannātha rejects the Abhinava-Gīta-Govinda (15th c.)** — Gajapati Puruṣottama Deva's imitation poem is laid overnight before Jagannātha, who keeps Jayadeva's original and casts the king's aside [regional] [oral tradition] _(actors: Puruṣottama Deva, Jagannātha, Jayadeva's text)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/jayadeva-and-the-gita-govinda.md) — (Odia temple tradition)
- **Jagannātha rides to Kāñcī for Puruṣottama Deva** — the Kāñcī-Kāverī legend: two divine horsemen precede the Gajapati's army, pay the milkmaid Māṇikā with a ring, and Padmāvatī weds the 'sweeper' king at Ratha Yātrā, attached to r. c. 1467–1497 [regional] [folk] _(actors: Jagannātha, Balabhadra, Puruṣottama Deva, Māṇikā, Padmāvatī)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/odia-literature-sarala-and-panchasakha.md) — (Mādaḷā Pāñji tradition; Odia kāvya retellings)
- **Jahāṅgīr and the founding of the Nadia Rāj** — delivered by Annadā's power at the imperial court, Bhavānanda receives honours and zamindari rights from Emperor Jahāṅgīr, the poem's charter-myth for Kṛṣṇacandra's dynasty _(actors: Bhavānanda Majumdār, Jahāṅgīr, Mān Siṃha, Annadā)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/annadamangal-bharatchandra.md) — (Annadāmaṅgal, Mānsiṃha khaṇḍa)
- **Jain Candraṇakhā variant composed** — Vimalasūri's Paümacariya (c. 4th–5th c. CE [scholarly]) recasts her as Candraṇakhā, mother of Śambūka accidentally slain by Lakṣmaṇa, with no mutilation _(actors: Vimalasūri, Candraṇakhā, Śambūka, Lakṣmaṇa)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/characters/shurpanakha.md) — (Paümacariya, Jain Rāmāyaṇa tradition)
- **Jain inversion of Sītā's birth composed** — Guṇabhadra's Uttarapurāṇa (9th c. CE) and Saṅghadāsa's Vasudevahiṇḍī record the Sītā-as-Mandodarī's-daughter variant [scholarly] _(actors: Guṇabhadra, Saṅghadāsa)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/characters/mandodari.md) — (Uttarapurāṇa; Vasudevahiṇḍī)
- **Jayaratha writes the Tantrāloka-viveka** — the indispensable commentary preserving the school's lost lineage history (c. 12th–13th c. CE [scholarly]) _(actors: Jayaratha)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/kashmir-shaiva-texts.md) — (Tantrāloka-viveka)
- **Jayatīrtha's conversion and sannyāsa** — the cavalry noble Dhoṇḍo Pant, awakened at the Bhīmā river to his past birth as the bullock that carried Madhva's books, takes ordination from Akṣobhya Tīrtha, mid-14th c. CE [scholarly] _(actors: Jayatīrtha, Akṣobhya Tīrtha)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/raghavendra-tirtha-and-the-dvaita-line.md) — (matha hagiography; Sharma, History of the Dvaita School)
- **Jyeṣṭhā Aṣṭamī and the Pandit exodus** — Kṣīr Bhavānī's Jyeṣṭhā Aṣṭamī mela at Tulmul becomes, after the 1990 Kashmiri Pandit exodus, the diaspora's chief pilgrimage-anchor and emblem of return _(actors: Kashmiri Pandits, Rājñā Devī)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/kashmir-tirthas-nilamata-geography.md) — (living Kashmiri Pandit practice) [regional]
- **Kabīr's anti-pilgrimage critique (15th c. CE)** — the Banaras weaver-sant mocks bathing-piety and reportedly dies at cursed Maghar to repudiate the Kāśī-death doctrine [oral tradition] _(actors: Kabīr)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/tirtha-and-tirtha-yatra.md) — (Kabīr corpus, Bījak/granthāvalī)
- **Kacchiappar composes the Kanda Purāṇam** — Kacchiyappa Sivāchāriyar of Kāñcīpuram renders and expands the Skanda Purāṇa's Śaṅkara-saṃhitā into the six-kāṇḍam Tamil epic of the Sūrapadma war _(actors: Kacchiyappa Sivāchāriyar)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/surapadma-samharam-kanda-puranam.md) — (Kanda Purāṇam; [scholarly] c. 1350–1400 CE)
- **Kadalī and the mendicant years** — the former king wanders as a begging jogī and passes through the enchantress-realm of Kadalī that also ensnares Matsyendranāth, resisting by his guru's power _(actors: Gopīcandra, Hāḍipā, the Kadalī sorceresses)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/gopichandra-maynamati-nath-epic.md) — (Gopīcandrer Gān; Nāth hagiography) [folk] [regional]
- **Kakshivat's danastuti to Svanaya Bhavya** — the poet Kakshivat itemizes king Svanaya Bhavya's gift of cows, chariots and teams on the Sindhu, prime documentary evidence of the Vedic poet-patron economy [scholarly] _(actors: Kakshivat, Svanaya Bhavya, Romasha)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/vedas/rigveda-samvada-and-secular-hymns.md) — (Rigveda 1.125–126)
- **Kalhaṇa opens the Rājataraṅgiṇī with the Nīlamata** — Writing in 1148–49 CE, Kalhaṇa names the Nīlamata among his sources and begins his chronicle inside its world — Satīsaras, Kaśyapa's desiccation, and the Gonanda kings _(actors: Kalhaṇa)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/puranas/nilamata-purana.md) — (Rājataraṅgiṇī 1.14–16, 1.25–27)
- **Kāḷi grieves her impaled son** — the foster-mother Kāḷi rages and grieves and cannot save her boy from the stake, the mother-goddess forced to witness her son's execution _(actors: Kāḷi, Kāttavarāyaṉ)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/kattavarayan-katai.md) — (Kaṭṭavarāyacuvāmi Kataippāṭal) [folk] [oral tradition]
- **Kālidāsa flourishes under the Guptas** — Kumārasambhava, Raghuvaṃśa, Abhijñānaśākuntala and Meghadūta composed c. 4th–5th c. CE [scholarly]; tradition instead seats him among Vikramāditya's navaratna (epoch 57 BCE) [late text] _(actors: Kālidāsa, Chandragupta II)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/classical-kavya-and-sanskrit-drama.md) — (Kālidāsa corpus, colophons)
- **Kalīla wa-Dimna rendered into Arabic** — Ibn al-Muqaffaʿ translates the Pahlavi Panchatantra, c. 750 CE [scholarly], naming it for the jackals Karaṭaka and Damanaka and founding the western branch _(actors: Ibn al-Muqaffaʿ, Kalīla, Dimna)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/panchatantra-hitopadesha-and-fable-literature.md) — (Kalīla wa-Dimna)
