- **Kaliṅga's maritime trading age remembered in the Tāpoi kathā [scholarly]** — Coastal Odisha's early-historic-to-medieval sea trade with Sri Lanka and Southeast Asia, run by the sādhaba merchants and their boitas, survives as folk memory in the Tāpoi ballad and the festival cluster of Boita Bandāṇa and Bali Jātrā _(actors: the sādhaba merchants of Kaliṅga)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/tapoi-khudurukuni-osha.md) — (histories of Kaliṅga maritime trade; Odia festival tradition)
- **Kaliyan the chieftain turns robber to feed devotees** — a Chōḷa warrior-chief of Ali Nāḍu, bound by Kumudavallī's vow to feed 1,008 Vaiṣṇavas daily, exhausts his treasury and takes to highway robbery to fund the sacred kitchen _(actors: Kaliyan/Parakāla, Kumudavallī)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/tirumangai-alvar.md) — (Guruparampara Prabhāvam, Parakāla chapters)
- **Kālketu founds the city of Gujarāṭ** — At Caṇḍī's command the hunter clears the forest, founds Gujarāṭ city with tax remissions for settlers, and institutes the goddess's worship _(actors: Kālketu, Caṇḍī, Murāri Śīl)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/chandimangal-kalketu-and-srimanta.md) — (Mukundarāma, Caṇḍīmaṅgal, Ākheṭika khaṇḍa)
- **Kalkuḍa and Kallurṭi deified as a paired daiva** — the wronged sculptor and his avenging sister die into godhood and are installed together, propitiated through the Tuḷu Bhūta-Kōla, with Kalkuḍa the tutelary daiva of the artisan castes _(actors: Kalkuḍa, Kallurṭi)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/kalkuda-kallurti.md) — (Tuḷu Bhūta-Kōla tradition) [regional]
- **Kalkuḍa raises the Kārkaḷa Bāhubali** — the master-sculptor Kalkuḍa carves and sets up the c. 42-ft monolith of Gommaṭeśvara (Bāhubali) at Kārkaḷa for king Vīra Pāṇḍya of the Sāntara dynasty _(actors: Kalkuḍa, Vīra Pāṇḍya)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/kalkuda-kallurti.md) — (Kalkuḍa paddana; Kārkaḷa statue, 1432 CE) [folk]
- **Kallurṭi burns the king's palace** — hearing of her brother's maiming, the fierce sister Kallurṭi takes a terrible form and sets fire to the royal palace of Kārkaḷa, consuming the line that crippled Kalkuḍa _(actors: Kallurṭi, Vīra Pāṇḍya)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/kalkuda-kallurti.md) — (Kalkuḍa–Kallurṭi paddana) [folk]
- **Kaḻuvēṟṟam — mounted on the impaling stake** — the god is raised alive onto the pointed kaḻu and dies impaled, the prolonged agony that forms the ritual and narrative core of the whole katai _(actors: Kāttavarāyaṉ)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/kattavarayan-katai.md) — (Masilamani-Meyer, The Tale of Kāttavarāyaṉ) [folk] [oral tradition]
- **Kamban's sympathetic Tamil Śūrpaṇakhā** — the Irāmāvatāram (12th c. CE [scholarly]) renders her wooing of Rāma in the beautiful Kāmavallī guise with courtly kāvya pathos and psychological interiority _(actors: Kamban, Śūrpaṇakhā/Kāmavallī, Rāma)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/characters/shurpanakha.md) — (Kamban, Irāmāvatāram, Āraṇya kāṇḍam)
- **Kāñcī conquered and Padmāvatī carried off** — led by the two divine riders, the second campaign breaks the Kāñcī army from Kāñcī to the Kāverī, and the emperor brings Padmāvatī back to Purī as war-prize _(actors: Puruṣottama Deva, Jagannātha, Balabhadra, Padmāvatī)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/kanchi-kaveri-jagannatha-legend.md) — (Mādaḷā Pāñji; Kāñcī-Kāverī) [regional]
- **Kāṇipākam as oath-court** — the shrine becomes a regional truth-ordeal where disputants swear before Varasiddhi Vināyaka on pain of a perjurer being swiftly stricken, a quasi-judicial oath acknowledged even beyond the temple _(actors: disputants, Varasiddhi Vināyaka)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/kanipakam-varasiddhi-vinayaka.md) — (temple tradition) [regional]
- **Kaṇṇaki ascends as Pattini from the Chera hill** — after fourteen days Kaṇṇaki rises bodily to heaven with Kōvalaṉ in a celestial chariot from Neṭuvēḷ Kuṉṟam, witnessed by the Kuṟavar hill-folk who resolve to worship her _(actors: Kaṇṇaki, Kōvalaṉ, the Kuṟavar)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/silappatikaram-kannagi-pattini.md) — (Cilappatikāram, Kuṉṟak kuravai)
- **Kaṇṇaki burns Madurai** — the vindicated Kaṇṇaki tears off her left breast and hurls it over the city, commanding Agni to spare the good and consume the wicked; the goddess Maturāpati reveals the past-life karma behind the tragedy _(actors: Kaṇṇaki, Agni, Maturāpati)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/silappatikaram-kannagi-pattini.md) — (Cilappatikāram, Vañciṉa mālai; Kaṭṭurai kātai)
- **Kaṇṇaki burns Madurai and ascends as Pattini** — Kaṇṇaki proves her executed husband Kōvalaṉ innocent, curses Madurai to flames, and is deified as the chastity-goddess Pattini, later merged with Kodungallur and Attukal Bhagavati _(actors: Kaṇṇaki, Kōvalaṉ, the Pāṇḍya king)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/bhadrakali-kerala-bhagavati.md) — (Cilappatikāram, Maturaikkāṇṭam–Vañcikkāṇṭam)
- **Kāśī-khaṇḍa codifies the Divodāsa cycle** — The Skanda Purāṇa's Kāśī-khaṇḍa, compiled c. 13th–14th century CE, fixes the Divodāsa–Śiva exile cycle in its received form [scholarly] _(actors: Kāśī-khaṇḍa compilers)_ → [details](../03-lineages-vamsha/kashi-line-divodasa.md) — (Skanda Purāṇa, Kāśī-khaṇḍa; Eck, Banaras)
- **Kāttavarāyaṉ fostered by Kāḷi** — a divine, cursed-born boy issuing from Śiva is given over to the black goddess Kāḷi, who rears him as her mischievous, low-born, trickster foster-son of the cremation-ground _(actors: Kāttavarāyaṉ, Kāḷi, Śiva)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/kattavarayan-katai.md) — (Kaṭṭavarāyacuvāmi Kataippāṭal) [folk] [oral tradition]
- **Khadga Tikkana's charge and death** — Nallasiddhi's Brahmin commander Khadga Tikkana attacks the Yadavas at Somasila, chases them to the Penna river, slays many heroes and falls in the field _(actors: Khadga Tikkana, Katamaraju)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/katamaraju-katha.md) — (Katamaraju Kathalu, Battle of Yerragaddapadu)
- **Khullanā's persecution and chastity ordeal** — The merchant's second wife, sent goat-herding in rags by co-wife Lahanā, wins Caṇḍī's protection and passes a public chastity ordeal by her grace _(actors: Khullanā, Lahanā, Durbalā, Caṇḍī, Dhanapati)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/chandimangal-kalketu-and-srimanta.md) — (Mukundarāma, Caṇḍīmaṅgal, Bāṇik khaṇḍa)
- **King Bhoja unearths Vikramāditya's lion-throne** — the Paramāra king of Dhārā finds the buried siṃhāsana whose 32 curse-bound statuettes each tell a tale of Vikramāditya's magnanimity, barring Bhoja until the thirty-second tale releases them _(actors: Bhoja, Vikramāditya, the 32 statuettes)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/brihatkatha-kathasaritsagara-and-vikramaditya-cycle.md) — (Siṃhāsana-dvātriṃśikā, frame) [late text]
- **King Sudarśana engages Viṣṇuśarman** — despairing of his ignorant sons at Pāṭaliputra, King Sudarśana entrusts them to the brahmin Viṣṇuśarman, who teaches nīti through four books of fables _(actors: Sudarśana, Viṣṇuśarman)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/hitopadesha.md) — (Hitopadeśa, prastāvikā)
- **Kottarakkara Thampuran creates Rāmanāṭṭam** — the danced Rāmāyaṇa cycle that grows into Kathakali, born (per legend) of the Zamorin's refusal to lend his Kṛṣṇanāṭṭam troupe (mid-17th c. CE) [oral tradition] _(actors: Rāja of Kottarakkara, Zamorin of Calicut)_ → [details](../09-artifacts-symbols-and-arts/classical-dance-forms-and-ritual-theatre.md) — (Kerala āṭṭakathā tradition)
- **Kōvalaṉ executed over the anklet at Madurai** — denounced by the thieving royal goldsmith, Kōvalaṉ is killed on the Pāṇḍya king Neṭuñceḻiyaṉ's untried order while selling Kaṇṇaki's jeweled anklet _(actors: Kōvalaṉ, the royal goldsmith, Neṭuñceḻiyaṉ)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/silappatikaram-kannagi-pattini.md) — (Cilappatikāram, Kolaikkaḷak kātai)
- **Kōvalaṉ weds Kaṇṇaki at Pukār and falls to Mātavi** — the merchant Kōvalaṉ marries Kaṇṇaki in the Chola port-capital, then squanders his fortune on the dancer Mātavi after her debut, leaving her carrying Maṇimēkalai _(actors: Kōvalaṉ, Kaṇṇaki, Mātavi)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/silappatikaram-kannagi-pattini.md) — (Cilappatikāram, Pukārkkāṇṭam)
- **Kṛṣṇa completes the 'dehi pada-pallavam' verse** — hagiography tells that Kṛṣṇa, in the bathing poet's form, wrote the half-verse asking Rādhā's foot upon his head and ate Padmāvatī's meal [oral tradition] _(actors: Jayadeva, Kṛṣṇa, Padmāvatī)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/jayadeva-and-the-gita-govinda.md) — (Bhakta-māla of Nābhādās, Jayadeva hagiography)
- **Kṛtyakalpataru compiled** — Bhaṭṭa Lakṣmīdhara, minister of the Gāhaḍavāla king Govindacandra of Kanauj, writes the earliest great encyclopedic dharma-digest in 14 kāṇḍas, the template for all later nibandhas _(actors: Lakṣmīdhara, Govindacandra)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/dharma-nibandhas-and-digests.md) — ([scholarly] c. 1110–1150 CE)
- **Kṣemarāja writes the Pratyabhijñāhṛdayam** — 20 sūtras with auto-commentary distilling recognition philosophy for the untrained, opening "citiḥ svatantrā viśva-siddhi-hetuḥ" (c. 1000–1050 CE [scholarly]) _(actors: Kṣemarāja)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/kashmir-shaiva-texts.md) — (Pratyabhijñāhṛdayam, sūtra 1)
- **Kṣemendra completes the Bṛhatkathāmañjarī (c. 1037 CE)** — the Kashmiri polymath produces a terse verse abridgement of the Bṛhatkathā, roughly a third the bulk of Somadeva's later ocean _(actors: Kṣemendra)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/brihatkatha-kathasaritsagara-and-vikramaditya-cycle.md) — (Bṛhatkathāmañjarī) [scholarly]
- **Kṣetrayya sings the Muvva Gopāla padams** — The wandering 17th-century Telugu padam-poet of Movva composes slow erotic-devotional padams to Kṛṣṇa as Muvva Gopāla, the core abhinaya repertoire of the later Bharatanāṭyam tradition _(actors: Kṣetrayya)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/haridasa-and-carnatic-bhakti.md) — (Wikipedia (Kshetrayya)) [scholarly] [disputed]
- **KSTS printing and the Lakshman Joo revival** — the Kashmir Series of Texts and Studies (1911 onward) prints the canon, and Swami Lakshman Joo (1907–1991) transmits it orally to modern lineages [scholarly] _(actors: Swami Lakshman Joo, Kashmiri paṇḍit editors)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/kashmir-shaiva-texts.md) — (KSTS editions; publication history)
- **Kulaśekhara Varman reforms Kūṭiyāṭṭam** — the Cera king reshapes Kerala's Sanskrit temple-theatre of the Cākyārs (c. 10th–11th c. CE) [scholarly] _(actors: Kulaśekhara Varman, the Cākyār community)_ → [details](../09-artifacts-symbols-and-arts/classical-dance-forms-and-ritual-theatre.md) — (Kerala kūṭiyāṭṭam tradition)
- **Kulaśekhara's snake-pot ordeal** — to clear the Bhāgavatas of a jewel-theft framed by jealous ministers, the Cēra king-saint thrusts his bare hand into a pot holding a live cobra and withdraws it unbitten _(actors: Kulaśekhara Āḻvār, the Śrīvaiṣṇava devotees, the ministers)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/alvar-hagiographies-guruparampara.md) — (Guruparampara Prabhāvam 6000-paḍi)
- **Kumārī Ghar built and the Kumārī Jātrā instituted** — the last Malla king of Kathmandu builds the Kumārī Bāhāl (1757 CE) and sets the living goddess's chariot procession within Indra Jātrā _(actors: Jaya Prakāśa Malla, the Royal Kumārī)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/nepal-kumari-taleju-machhindranath.md) — (Nepalese chronicles; Kathmandu tradition) [scholarly]
- **Kumbakonam sung into the Tevāram** — the Nāyaṉār saints glorify the town's Śiva shrines in the 6th–9th c. hymn-canon, making it a Paadal Petra temple-city of Chōḷa Nāḍu _(actors: Appar, Sambandar, Sundarar)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/kumbakonam-adi-kumbheshwara-mahamaham.md) — (Tevāram, Tirumurai 1–7) [scholarly]
- **Kurukṣetra-milana and the Ratha-yātrā** — Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavas read the Purī chariot festival as this reunion re-enacted, the gopīs drawing Kṛṣṇa from Dvārakā back to Vṛndāvana, with Caitanya reliving Rādhā's ecstasy _(actors: Caitanya, Jagannātha, Rūpa Gosvāmī)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/kurukshetra-reunion-gopis.md) — (Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Madhya 13; Padyāvalī 383)
- **Kusumā Mālin vindicates and frees Salhesh** — The flower-woman Kusumā Mālin, who loves Salhesh, exposes the real thief through her wit and magic; Salhesh is freed and restored to honour and kingship, their cross-caste love left unconsummated in many tellings _(actors: Kusumā Mālin, Rājā Salhesh)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/maithili-oral-epics-salhesh-dina-bhadri.md) — (Rājā Salhes gāthā) [oral tradition]
- **Lakṣmīdhara compiles the Tīrthavivecanakāṇḍa (c. 1110–1150 CE)** — the first great dharmaśāstra digest of pilgrimage law, sifting māhātmya claims into rules of yātrā [scholarly] _(actors: Lakṣmīdhara, the Gāhaḍavāla court)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/tirtha-and-tirtha-yatra.md) — (Kṛtyakalpataru, Tīrthavivecanakāṇḍa)
- **Lalitāditya builds Mārtāṇḍa** — the Kārkoṭa king Lalitāditya Muktāpīḍa monumentalizes Kashmir's great Sūrya temple at Mattan, c. 725–756 CE _(actors: Lalitāditya Muktāpīḍa, Sūrya/Mārtāṇḍa)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/kashmir-tirthas-nilamata-geography.md) — (Rājataraṅgiṇī 4; art-historical dating) [scholarly]
- **Living samadhi of Ramdev at Runicha (Ramdevra)** — Ramdev takes jivit samadhi at Runicha, traditionally Bhadrapada shukla 11, V.S. 1442 (c. 1385 CE), aged thirty-three; the samadhi becomes the cult's center _(actors: Ramdev)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/ramdev-pir.md) — (Ramdevra sthala tradition) [folk] [disputed]
- **Madhusūdana's bhakti reconciliation** — the strict non-dualist Kṛṣṇa-devotee writes the Bhakti-rasāyana and the Gītā-commentary Gūḍhārtha-dīpikā, and (by legend) petitions Akbar's court to arm the Daśanāmī Nāgas _(actors: Madhusūdana Sarasvatī, Kṛṣṇa)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/later-advaita-acharyas.md) — (Bhakti-rasāyana) [folk]
- **Māgha composes the Śiśupālavadha** — 20-canto mahākāvya on Kṛṣṇa's beheading of Śiśupāla at the rājasūya, c. late 7th c. CE [scholarly] _(actors: Māgha)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/classical-kavya-and-sanskrit-drama.md) — (Śiśupālavadha 1–20)
- **Mahendravarman's cave-temples** — the Pallava king Mahendravarman I cuts rock-temples with dedicatory inscriptions into the Tiruchirāppaḷḷi rock, the earliest hard evidence of worship on the hill _(actors: Mahendravarman I)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/tiruchirappalli-rockfort-thayumanavar.md) — (Pallava inscriptions, early 7th c. CE) [scholarly]
- **Mahiṣāsura-mardinī radio Chaṇḍī-pāṭh begins (early 1930s CE)** — Bīrendra Kṛṣṇa Bhadra's dawn recitation of the Devī Māhātmya on All India Radio becomes the canonical acoustic opening of Mahālayā and Devī-pakṣa _(actors: Bīrendra Kṛṣṇa Bhadra, All India Radio)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/durga-puja-bengal.md) — (AIR broadcast history) [scholarly]
- **Majapahit conquers Bali (1343 CE)** — Gajah Mada subdues the island, grafting Javanese court religion, triwangsa caste usage and temple style onto the older Balinese base [scholarly] _(actors: Gajah Mada, Majapahit court)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/balinese-hinduism-agama-hindu-dharma.md) — (Nāgarakṛtāgama; Balinese babad)
- **Māmunigaḷ's year-long Īḍu kālakṣepam and the Śrīśailēśa taniyan** — Raṅganātha appears as a boy and recites the invocatory verse honouring his teacher, c. 1430s [traditional] _(actors: Maṇavāḷa Māmunigaḷ, Raṅganātha)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/vedanta-desika-and-later-srivaishnava-acharyas.md) — (Wikipedia, Manavala Mamunigal)
- **Man-eating leopard of Rudraprayag killed (1926 CE)** — Jim Corbett ends the leopard's eight-year predation on Kedarnath-road pilgrims [scholarly] _(actors: Jim Corbett)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/panch-prayag-alaknanda-confluences.md) — (Corbett, The Man-Eating Leopard of Rudraprayag, 1948)
- **Mānaveda composes the Kṛṣṇagīti** — the Zamorin's eight-night Kṛṣṇanāṭṭam cycle instituted as votive offering at Guruvayur (1650s CE) _(actors: Zamorin Mānaveda, Vilvamaṅgalam)_ → [details](../09-artifacts-symbols-and-arts/classical-dance-forms-and-ritual-theatre.md) — (Kṛṣṇagīti; Guruvayur temple tradition)
- **Mānaveda's peacock-feather vision and the Kṛṣṇagīti (c. 1652 CE)** — granted a vision of the child Kṛṣṇa through Vilvamaṅgalam, prince Mānaveda is left holding a peacock feather and creates Kṛṣṇanāṭṭam, the eight-night dance-drama offered at the temple _(actors: Mānaveda, Vilvamaṅgalam Svāmiyār, Kṛṣṇa)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/guruvayur-krishna-kshetra.md) — (Kṛṣṇagīti; Kṛṣṇanāṭṭam tradition) [folk]
- **Māndappan driven from home** — the wayward, quarrelsome Chekavar youth Māndappan is expelled by his father Kumarachan and leaves North Malabar for his uncle's house in Coorg (Kodagu) _(actors: Māndappan / Kathivanur Veeran, Kumarachan)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/kathivanur-veeran.md) — (Kathivanur Veeran thottam-pāṭṭu)
- **Māṇikcandra taken by Yama** — King Māṇikcandra of Rangpur dies at the hands of Yama's messengers despite his deathless yoginī-queen's power, setting the epic's premise that even a king cannot escape death without yoga _(actors: Māṇikcandra, Maynāmatī, Yama's messengers)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/gopichandra-maynamati-nath-epic.md) — (Māṇik Chandra Rājār Gān, Grierson 1878) [folk]
- **Manusmṛti enjoins the Brahma-muhūrta** — the dharmaśāstra rule 'brāhme muhūrte budhyeta' fixes pre-dawn waking as a daily duty, c. 200 BCE–200 CE [scholarly] _(actors: Manu (attributed))_ → [details](../00-time-and-cosmology/muhurta-and-auspicious-timing.md) — (Manusmṛti 4.92)
- **Masilamani-Meyer documents the tale** — Eveline Masilamani-Meyer's critical study, edition and translation, The Tale of Kāttavarāyaṉ (Harrassowitz, 2004), records the ballad and its impalement cult for scholarship _(actors: Eveline Masilamani-Meyer)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/kattavarayan-katai.md) — (Masilamani-Meyer, The Tale of Kāttavarāyaṉ, 2004) [scholarly]
- **Mass Kanwar Yatra explosion (c. 1980s–2000s)** — Ethnographers date the north-western yatra's growth from a small sadhu-and-elder practice into a many-million annual pilgrimage to the late 20th century [scholarly] _(actors: Kanwariya pilgrims)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/kanwar-yatra-and-shravan-shiva-observances.md) — (Vikash Singh, Uprising of the Fools, 2017)
- **Maynāmatī's death-prophecy** — the yoginī Maynāmatī, disciple of Gorakṣanāth, foresees that her son King Gopīcandra will die within days unless he abandons throne and wives to be initiated as a jogī _(actors: Maynāmatī, Gopīcandra, Gorakṣanāth)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/gopichandra-maynamati-nath-epic.md) — (Gopīcandrer Gān) [folk]
- **Medhātithi condemns widow-burning (c. 9th–10th c. CE)** — The great commentator on Manu rules anvārohaṇa to be forbidden suicide, comparing it to the effective-but-adharmic Śyena sacrifice — the strongest internal śāstric rejection of satī [scholarly] _(actors: Medhātithi)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/sati-jauhar-and-the-immolation-rites.md) — (Medhātithi on Manusmṛti 5.157)
- **Medieval digests codify the tithi-vratas** — Hemādri's Caturvarga-cintāmaṇi (13th c.) and the Nirṇayasindhu (17th c.) codify these observances, quoting a fluid "Bhaviṣyottara"; the kathās as recited are 19th–20th-c. printed pothīs [scholarly] _(actors: Hemādri, Kamalākara Bhaṭṭa)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/vrata-kathas-catalog.md) — (Caturvarga-cintāmaṇi; Nirṇayasindhu) [scholarly]
- **Mehaji Mangaliya falls in battle on Janmashtami** — the Mangaliya Rajput hero of the panch-pir roster dies fighting on Krishna's birth-day, on which his fair at Bapini is held (trad. 14th c.) [folk] _(actors: Mehaji Mangaliya, Kirad Kabra)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/tejaji.md) — (Bapini shrine tradition)
- **Melpathur completes the Nārāyaṇīyam (27/28 Nov 1586 CE)** — the paralysed Melpathur Nārāyaṇa Bhaṭṭatiri sings one daśaka a day for 100 days before the deity, is cured at the closing vision, and dates the work by the kali-dina chronogram āyur-ārogya-saukhyam _(actors: Melpathur Nārāyaṇa Bhaṭṭatiri, Acyuta Piṣāroṭi, Ezhuttacchan, Guruvāyūrappan)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/guruvayur-krishna-kshetra.md) — (Nārāyaṇīyam, daśaka 100) [scholarly]
- **Mhaskoba follows Kāmājī to Vīr** — Kāḷ-Bhairava migrates from Sonāri in the Bhīmā valley to Vīr in the wake of his Dhangar devotee Kāmājī, where his blood-taking cult takes root _(actors: Mhaskoba/Kāḷ-Bhairava, Kāmājī Dhangar)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/dhangar-oral-epics-biroba-mhaskoba-dhuloba.md) — (Mhaskoba of Vīr; Sontheimer, Between Ghost and God)
- **Militarized akharas crystallize** — the armed ascetic regiments of sannyāsins, including the nāgā orders, take their standardized form by the 18th century [scholarly], despite the traditional 8th-c. Śaṅkara attribution _(actors: Daśanāmī and Bairāgī ascetic orders)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/sannyasa-and-the-renunciate-tradition.md) — (Kumbh Melā akhara records)
- **Mitākṣarā written** — Vijñāneśvara, at the Cālukya court of Vikramāditya VI of Kalyāṇī, composes the commentary on the Yājñavalkya-smṛti that founds the birthright (janma-svatva) school of Hindu inheritance law _(actors: Vijñāneśvara, Vikramāditya VI)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/dharma-nibandhas-and-digests.md) — ([scholarly] c. 1076–1127 CE)
- **Modern recovery of the Nīlamata** — Bühler's 1877 manuscript report signals the text's importance, de Vreese's critical edition follows in 1936, and Ved Kumari Ghai publishes the standard study and translation in 1968–73 [scholarly] _(actors: G. Bühler, K. de Vreese, Ved Kumari Ghai)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/puranas/nilamata-purana.md) — (Bühler 1877; de Vreese 1936; Ghai 1968–73)
- **Mokṣopāya composed in Kashmir (c. 950 CE)** — An anonymous Kashmiri author composes the Mokṣopāya, the earliest recoverable recension of the Yoga-Vāsiṣṭha, later Vedānticized into the vulgate [scholarly] _(actors: anonymous Kashmiri author)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/yoga-vasishtha.md) — (Mokṣopāya project, Slaje/Hanneder)
- **Mpu Kuturan institutes the kahyangan tiga (c. 11th c.)** — the Javanese sage organizes every Balinese village around three temples (Pura Puseh, Pura Desa, Pura Dalem), unifying the island's sects [traditional] _(actors: Mpu Kuturan, Udayana, Mahendradatta)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/balinese-hinduism-agama-hindu-dharma.md) — (Balinese lontar/babad tradition)
- **Mudiyettu inscribed by UNESCO (2010 CE)** — The ritual dance-drama of the Dārika-vadham, performed by Marar and Kuruppu communities in central Kerala's Bhagavati kāvus, is inscribed on UNESCO's Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity _(actors: Marar and Kuruppu performer communities, UNESCO)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/bhadrakali-kerala-bhagavati.md) — (UNESCO ICH listing, 2010) [scholarly]
- **Mukundarāma Kavikaṅkaṇa composes the canonical Caṇḍīmaṅgal** — Fleeing revenue oppression at Dāmunyā, Mukundarāma is commissioned by the goddess in a dream and composes the masterwork under Bāṅkurā Rāy's patronage (c. 1590s) [scholarly] _(actors: Mukundarāma Cakravartī, Caṇḍī, Bāṅkurā Rāy, Raghunāth Rāy)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/chandimangal-kalketu-and-srimanta.md) — (Mukundarāma, Caṇḍīmaṅgal, ātma-kāhinī prologue)
- **Murugan catches the falling saint** — before he strikes the ground Murugan arrests the fall, appears in grace, and cures his wasting disease _(actors: Murugan, Aruṇagirinātar)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/arunagirinathar-tiruppugazh.md) — (hagiographic tradition)
- **Muttuppaṭṭaṉ meets the Chakkiliyar sisters** — a wandering brahmin in the Tirunelveli country is seized by love for the two daughters of a leather-worker, Bommakkā and Timmakkā, who mockingly refuse him _(actors: Muttuppaṭṭaṉ, Bommakkā, Timmakkā)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/muttuppattan-katai.md) — (Muttuppaṭṭaṉ Katai, vil pāṭṭu)
- **Muttuppaṭṭaṉ renounces brahminhood and works leather** — the brahmin accepts every condition, takes up the polluting craft, tans hides and stitches sandals, living as a cobbler among cobblers _(actors: Muttuppaṭṭaṉ)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/muttuppattan-katai.md) — (Muttuppaṭṭaṉ Katai, vil pāṭṭu)
- **Nakhshabī casts the parrot into Persian** — Ziyāʾ al-Dīn Nakhshabī reworks the cycle as the Ṭūṭī-nāma c. 1330–1335 CE, compressing the seventy tales into fifty-two nights on an earlier Persian Javāhir al-asmār (c. 1313–15) _(actors: Nakhshabī, ʿImād ibn Muḥammad)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/shuka-saptati.md) — (Ṭūṭī-nāma)
- **Nakhshabī's Ṭūṭī-nāma** — the Śukasaptati's seventy parrot-tales adapted into Persian, 1330 CE [scholarly] _(actors: Nakhshabī, the parrot, Prabhāvatī)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/panchatantra-hitopadesha-and-fable-literature.md) — (Ṭūṭī-nāma, after Śukasaptati)
- **Nambi Āṇṭār Nambi expands the saint-roster (c. 10th–11th c. CE)** — the Tiruttoṇṭar Tiruvandāti, under Rājarāja I's canon-recovery, fixes the count toward the canonical 63 [scholarly] _(actors: Nambiyāṇḍār Nambi, Rājarāja I)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/periya-puranam-63-nayanmar-legend-map.md) — (Tiruttoṇṭar Tiruvandāti)
- **Nandā becomes royal tutelary goddess of the hill kingdoms** — the Katyūrī (c. 8th–12th c. CE) and then Chand and Garhwal Parmār dynasties adopt Nandā as kul-devī, installing her at Almora and Kot Bhrāmarī _(actors: Katyūrī kings, Chand rājās, Garhwal Parmār rājās)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/nanda-devi-and-uttarakhand-deities.md) — (Almora and Kot Bhrāmarī sthala traditions [regional] [scholarly])
- **Nandanar the Pulaiyar devotee** — a bonded agricultural labourer of the Pulaiyar community at Ādhaṉūr, barred by caste from Śiva temples, serves the Lord from the threshold with leather, drum-skins and garlands _(actors: Nandanar / Tirunāḷaippōvār)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/nandanar-tirunalaippovar.md) — (Periya Purāṇam, Tirunāḷaippōvār Nāyaṉār Purāṇam no. 18)
- **Nārāyaṇ Mahārāj institutes the pādukā procession (late 17th c.)** — Tukārām's youngest son begins carrying the sandals of Tukārām and Jñāneśvar in joint procession to Pandharpur, seeding the pālkhī tradition _(actors: Nārāyaṇ Mahārāj, Tukārām's and Jñāneśvar's pādukās)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/pandharpur-vitthala-wari.md) — (Vārkarī tradition; Deleury, The Cult of Vithoba) [scholarly]
- **Nārāyaṇa Bhaṭṭa writes the Tristhalīsetu (16th c. CE)** — the 'Bridge to the Three Cities' (Kāśī, Prayāga, Gayā) caps the scholastic jurisprudence of tīrtha-yātrā, codifying muṇḍana, fasting and the on-foot rule [scholarly] _(actors: Nārāyaṇa Bhaṭṭa)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/tirtha-and-tirtha-yatra.md) — (Tristhalīsetu)
- **Nārāyaṇa composes the Hitopadeśa** — the Bengal poet Nārāyaṇa, under his patron Dhavala-candra, reworks the Pañcatantra and Kāmandaki's Nītisāra into a four-book Sanskrit nīti-kathā _(actors: Nārāyaṇa, Dhavala-candra)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/hitopadesha.md) — (Hitopadeśa, preamble; [scholarly] c. 800–1373 CE)
- **Narayana Guru consecrates the Aruvippuram Śiva** — The Ezhava sage installs a Śiva-liṅga himself in 1888 — 'my Śiva, an Ezhava Śiva' — founding Kerala's anti-caste temple movement, later organized as the SNDP Yogam (1903) under the creed 'one caste, one religion, one God for man' _(actors: Narayana Guru; Dr. Palpu; Kumaran Asan)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/modern-teachers-and-reform.md) — (Wikipedia (Narayana Guru); Britannica) [scholarly]
- **Naunidhirāma's Sāroddhāra digest and its 1911 English translation** — the later digest quoting a lost recension carries the tale as its ch. 7 and, via Wood & Subrahmanyam (1911), becomes the globally circulated form of the story [scholarly] _(actors: Naunidhirāma, Ernest Wood, S. V. Subrahmanyam)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/babhruvahana-and-the-preta.md) — (Garuḍa-Purāṇa-Sāroddhāra, ch. 7)
- **Nirartha founds Tanah Lot** — the reformer-priest Dang Hyang Nirartha seeds Bali's chain of clifftop sea-temples to the sea-god Baruṇa, including the tidal-rock shrine of Tanah Lot _(actors: Dang Hyang Nirartha, Baruṇa)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/greater-india-sacred-geography.md) — ([scholarly] 16th c. CE; [regional])
- **Nirṇayasindhu completed** — Kamalākara Bhaṭṭa of Banaras produces the standard authority on kāla-nirṇaya, fixing the correct calendrical time for every festival, fast and saṃskāra _(actors: Kamalākara Bhaṭṭa)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/dharma-nibandhas-and-digests.md) — (Nirṇayasindhu, 1612 CE)
- **Nishān padayātrā and sawāmaṇī** — the living cults take shape: vow-keepers carry the triangular _nishān_ flag on foot to Sālāsar at the Chaitra and Āśvin Pūrṇimā fairs, and fulfilled petitioners at both shrines host _sawāmaṇī_ feasts in the auspicious savā (1¼) measure _(actors: pilgrims, Bālājī)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/mehandipur-salasar-balaji-hanuman-shrines.md) — (Sālāsar and Mehandīpur traditions) [regional]
- **Nityānanda narrates the legend at the shrine** — travelling with the Bengali devotees toward Purī, Nityānanda Prabhu recounts the whole Sākṣi-Gopāla history at the temple, fixing it as a signature Gauḍīya tale later redacted by Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja c. 1600 CE _(actors: Nityānanda Prabhu, Bengali devotees)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/sakshi-gopal-witness-krishna.md) — (Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Madhya 5)
- **Oldest Svasthānī manuscript (1573 CE)** — the earliest dated copy preserves a short Sanskrit core that expands over four centuries into the 31-chapter Nepali household recension _(actors: Nepali scribes, Newar/Parbatiyā tradition)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/swasthani-brata-katha.md) — (Birkenholtz, Reciting the Goddess, 2018)
- **Olivelle's critical edition (2005)** — Patrick Olivelle reconstructs an earlier text behind the medieval vulgate and dates the composition to the 2nd–3rd century CE as a single-redactor ring-structured work _(actors: Patrick Olivelle, Medhātithi, Kullūka)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/manusmriti-map.md) — (Olivelle, *Manu's Code of Law*, 2005)
- **Ōtēnan is felled by the gun** — invincible with the sword, the young Tacōḷi hero is at last killed only by treachery with a matchlock firearm, the ballad's bitter comment that the gun ends the age of the blade _(actors: Tacōḷi Ōtēnan)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/vadakkan-pattukal-northern-ballads.md) — (Vaṭakkan Pāṭṭukaḷ, Tacōḷi cycle)
- **Pādukā-sahasram composed in one night** — Deśika answers a poet's challenge with 1,008 verses on Raṅganātha's sandals, including picture-pattern verses [traditional] _(actors: Vedānta Deśika, Raṅganātha)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/vedanta-desika-and-later-srivaishnava-acharyas.md) — (Wikipedia, Paduka Sahasram)
- **Panchatantra core composed** — Viṣṇuśarman's five nīti-fable books (frame: teaching King Amaraśakti's three dull sons in six months) take shape, c. 200 BCE–300 CE [scholarly] _(actors: Viṣṇuśarman, Amaraśakti, Karaṭaka, Damanaka)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/panchatantra-hitopadesha-and-fable-literature.md) — (Panchatantra, Kathāmukha)
- **Panchatantra embedded in the Kathāsaritsāgara** — Somadeva of Kashmir retells the five tantras inside the Bṛhatkathā ocean-of-story, c. 1070 CE [scholarly] _(actors: Somadeva, Guṇāḍhya, Naravāhanadatta)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/panchatantra-hitopadesha-and-fable-literature.md) — (Kathāsaritsāgara, Lambhaka X)
- **Pāṇḍuraṅga-māhātmya recensions composed** — the Sanskrit sthala-purāṇa of Pandharpur is transmitted in Skanda and Padma Purāṇa recensions as a medieval glorification of the living cult _(actors: anonymous māhātmya redactors)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/pandharpur-vitthala-wari.md) — (Pāṇḍuraṅga-māhātmya) [late text] [scholarly]
- **Panjurli and Guliga reach a global audience** — the film *Kantara* (2022) stages a climactic Guliga Bhūta-Kōla, carrying the Tuḷu daivas and their possession-ritual to worldwide attention _(actors: Panjurli, Guliga)_ → [details](../08-beings-and-bestiary/panjurli-guliga-tulu-daivas.md) — (Bhūta-Kōla tradition; Kantara, 2022) [regional]
- **Paranjōti sacks Vātāpi** — the Pallava general Paranjōti leads Narasiṃhavarman I's army to burn the Cāḷukya capital Vātāpi (Bādāmi), then renounces war to serve Śiva at Tirucceṅkāṭṭaṅkuḍi as Ciṟuttoṇṭar _(actors: Paranjōti / Ciṟuttoṇṭar, Narasiṃhavarman I, Pulakeśin II)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/siruthondar-nayanar.md) — (Periya Purāṇam, Ciṟuttoṇṭa Purāṇam; [scholarly] c. 642 CE)
- **Parāśara Bhaṭṭar heads Śrīraṅgam** — Kūrattāḻvān's son succeeds Rāmānuja's line (c. 1122–1174 [traditional]) and converts the Advaitin Nañjīyar _(actors: Parāśara Bhaṭṭar, Nañjīyar)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/vedanta-desika-and-later-srivaishnava-acharyas.md) — (Wikipedia, Parasara Bhattar)
- **Pārijātāpaharaṇamu and Bhāmākalāpam** — the episode becomes a literary and dance classic in Nandi Timmana's Telugu prabandha at Kṛṣṇadevarāya's court and in Siddhendra Yogi's Kuchipudi Bhāmākalāpam _(actors: Nandi Timmana, Kṛṣṇadevarāya, Siddhendra Yogi)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/parijata-harana.md) — (Telugu kāvya and Kuchipudi tradition) [scholarly]
- **Periya Tirumoḻi begins with 'Vāḍiṉēṉ vāḍi'** — the redeemed saint pours out six works of ~1,253 verses, the 1,084-verse Periya Tirumoḻi opening with the world-weary discovery of the Nārāyaṇa name _(actors: Tirumaṅgai Āḻvār)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/tirumangai-alvar.md) — (Periya Tirumoḻi 1.1)
- **Periyāḻvār's Tiruppallāṇḍu on Garuḍa** — after winning the Madurai para-tattva debate, Viṣṇucittar sees Viṣṇu appear Garuḍa-mounted and, fearing for God like a parent for a child, sings the benediction that opens the Prabandham _(actors: Periyāḻvār/Viṣṇucittar, king Vallabhadeva, Viṣṇu on Garuḍa)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/alvar-hagiographies-guruparampara.md) — (Divya Sūri Caritam, Periyāḻvār account)
- **Persian Jūg Bāsisht translated for Prince Salīm (1597 CE)** — Niẓām al-Dīn Pānīpatī renders the Laghu into Persian for the future Jahāngīr; Mīr Findiriskī and Dārā Shukoh's circle continue the Persian reception [scholarly] _(actors: Niẓām al-Dīn Pānīpatī, Prince Salīm/Jahāngīr, Mīr Findiriskī, Dārā Shukoh)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/yoga-vasishtha.md) — (Jūg Bāsisht, 1597)
- **Piḷḷai Lokācārya composes the eighteen rahasya-granthas** — Śrīvacana Bhūṣaṇa, Mumukṣuppaḍi and Tattva-traya fix the doctrine of causeless grace, late 13th–early 14th c. [scholarly] _(actors: Piḷḷai Lokācārya)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/vedanta-desika-and-later-srivaishnava-acharyas.md) — (Wikipedia, Pillai Lokacharya)
- **Po Nagar goddess sanctuary of Champa** — the Cham enshrine Yang Pô Inâ Nagar at Nha Trang, identified in Sanskrit as Bhagavatī/Umā and depicted as Mahiṣāsura-mardinī _(actors: Yang Pô Inâ Nagar, Bhagavatī)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/greater-india-sacred-geography.md) — ([scholarly]; [regional])
- **Poonthanam and the Jñānappāna (c. 1547–1640 CE)** — grieving his infant son, Poonthanam takes the child Kṛṣṇa as his own and composes the Malayalam bhakti classic, the Lord declaring his bhakti dearer than Melpathur's vibhakti _(actors: Poonthanam Nambūdiri, Melpathur, Guruvāyūrappan)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/guruvayur-krishna-kshetra.md) — (Jñānappāna; Kerala oral tradition) [folk]
- **Poṭṭan Theyyam of the meleri** — the North-Malabar kāvu theyyam in which the possessed performer falls and rolls on a bed of live embers, embodying the casteless Śiva across caste lines _(actors: theyyam performer, kāvu community)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/pottan-theyyam.md) — (Kaliyāṭṭam tradition)
- **Prambanan raised to the Trimūrti** — the Sañjaya-Mataram court builds Indonesia's largest Hindu temple, three shrines to Śiva, Brahmā and Viṣṇu with their vāhanas and Rāmāyaṇa reliefs _(actors: Rakai Pikatan, Śiva, Brahmā, Viṣṇu)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/greater-india-sacred-geography.md) — ([scholarly] mid-9th c. CE)
- **Pratāparudra Deva inscription mandates the Gīta-Govinda at Purī (1499 CE)** — a Jaya-Vijaya doorway inscription orders that only Jayadeva's songs be performed before Jagannātha, codifying the nightly aṣṭapadī sevā [scholarly] _(actors: Pratāparudra Deva, Jagannātha temple māhārīs)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/jayadeva-and-the-gita-govinda.md) — (Jagannātha temple inscription, 1499 CE)
- **Pratimā-lakṣaṇa — the iconography of the gods** — Khaṇḍa III's image-making canon describes the multi-armed Viṣṇu, the four-faced Vaikuṇṭha, Sūrya, Śiva and the Goddess — a prime early source used to read Gupta and post-Gupta sculpture _(actors: Viṣṇudharmottara redactors)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/puranas/vishnudharmottara-purana.md) — (Viṣṇudharmottara Purāṇa, Khaṇḍa III)
- **Present Ramdevra temple built under Ganga Singh** — the current temple over Ramdev's samadhi is completed in 1931 CE under Maharaja Ganga Singh of Bikaner _(actors: Maharaja Ganga Singh)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/ramdev-pir.md) — (Ramdevra sthala tradition) [scholarly]
- **Printed pothīs spread the pūjā across India (19th–20th c.)** — mass-printed vernacular pothīs in Hindi, Marathi, Gujarati and Telugu make the Satyanārāyaṇa Pūjā the most widely performed household kathā of modern Hinduism _(actors: pothī publishers, household performers)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/satyanarayana-vrata-katha.md) — (printed vrata-kathā pothīs, 19th–20th c.) [scholarly]
- **Prosperity and marriage in Coorg** — the reformed Māndappan thrives as a merchant in Kodagu and weds his cross-cousin Chemmarathi _(actors: Māndappan, Chemmarathi)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/kathivanur-veeran.md) — (Kathivanur Veeran thottam-pāṭṭu)
- **Pṛthvī Nārāyaṇ Śāh takes Kathmandu at Indra Jātrā and receives the Kumārī's tikā** — the Gorkhā conqueror captures the city on the festival day of 1768 CE and takes the living goddess's blessing, transferring Taleju's royal mandate to the Śāh dynasty _(actors: Pṛthvī Nārāyaṇ Śāh, the Royal Kumārī, Jaya Prakāśa Malla)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/nepal-kumari-taleju-machhindranath.md) — (Nepalese chronicles; popular tradition) [scholarly]
- **Pūcalār Builds a Temple in His Mind** — the penniless brāhmaṇa of Tiruniṉṟavūr, unable to afford stone, raises Śiva a complete temple entirely in his mind — foundation, walls, vimāna and liṅga imagined in Āgamic order _(actors: Pūcalār Nāyaṉār, Śiva)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/pusalar-nayanar-mind-temple.md) — (Periya Purāṇam, Pūcalār Purāṇam)
- **Pura Besakih, Bali's Mother Temple** — a complex of ~23 temples on the volcano Gunung Agung, treated as the island's terrestrial Meru and chief of the Sad-Kahyangan sanctuaries _(actors: Balinese Hindus)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/greater-india-sacred-geography.md) — ([regional])
- **Pūrṇabhadra's Jain recension completed** — the fullest surviving Panchatantra, 1199 CE [scholarly], today's de-facto vulgate and basis of most modern translations _(actors: Pūrṇabhadra)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/panchatantra-hitopadesha-and-fable-literature.md) — (Panchatantra, Pūrṇabhadra recension colophon)
- **Puruṣottama Deva's southern war** — insulted as a 'sweeper' before Jagannātha's chariot and denied the Vidyānagara princess, the Gajapati king marches south, is aided by Jagannātha and Balabhadra as two warriors, and conquers the city _(actors: Puruṣottama Deva, Jagannātha, Balabhadra)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/sakshi-gopal-witness-krishna.md) — (Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Madhya 5; Gajapati record, r. c. 1467–1497 CE)
- **Rāghavendra enters the bṛndāvana alive (jīva-samādhi)** — on a pre-announced day in 1671 CE [scholarly] the saint sits in meditation as the tomb-shrine is built over him, vowed to remain alive within it for 700 years [traditional] _(actors: Rāghavendra Tīrtha)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/raghavendra-tirtha-and-the-dvaita-line.md) — (Rāghavendra Vijaya tradition; Mantrālayam maṭha tradition)
- **Rāghavendra Tīrtha's sannyāsa at Tanjore** — the vīṇā-playing scholar Veṅkaṭanātha, chosen by Sudhīndra Tīrtha and commanded in a dream by the goddess of learning, is ordained in 1621 CE [scholarly] _(actors: Rāghavendra Tīrtha, Sudhīndra Tīrtha)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/raghavendra-tirtha-and-the-dvaita-line.md) — (Rāghavendra Vijaya)
- **Rāhu-kāla, Yamagaṇḍa, Gulika grid and Choghaḍiyā enter almanac practice** — the eightfold weekday grid of inauspicious hours and the western-Indian choghaḍiyā system, unattested in classical siddhāntas, spread as late regional pañcāṅga practice [late text] [regional] [scholarly] _(actors: Rāhu, Yama, Gulika/Māndi (as personifications))_ → [details](../00-time-and-cosmology/muhurta-and-auspicious-timing.md) — (modern pañcāṅga practice; Drik Panchang)
- **Rājā Salhesh enters Mithilā painting via the Dusādh godnā school (late 20th c.)** — Dalit painters of the Madhubani region, most famously Chano Devi with Raudi Paswan, make the Salhesh cycle the central subject of their tattoo-derived godnā style, placing the Dusādh epic beside Rāma and Kṛṣṇa on paper _(actors: Chano Devi, Raudi Paswan, Dusādh painters)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/maithili-oral-epics-salhesh-dina-bhadri.md) — (Mithilā-painting scholarship on the godnā school) [scholarly]
- **Rājarāja I settles 400 dancing-women at Thanjavur** — the Cōḻa emperor endows taḷicceri peṇḍugaḷ around the Bṛhadīśvara temple, the great epigraphic record of the devadāsī institution (c. 1004–1010 CE) [scholarly] _(actors: Rājarāja I Cōḻa, the temple devadāsīs)_ → [details](../09-artifacts-symbols-and-arts/classical-dance-forms-and-ritual-theatre.md) — (Thanjavur temple inscriptions)
- **Ramalinga Vallalar founds the worship of the Grace-Light** — The Tamil saint-poet of the Tiruvaruṭpā establishes the casteless Samarasa Suddha Sanmārga (1865), the Vadalur free feeding-house (1867) and the Satya Jñāna Sabhai (1872) where God is worshipped as Aruṭperuñjōti, a lit lamp behind veils, before vanishing at Mettukuppam in 1874 _(actors: Ramalinga Swamigal (Vallalar))_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/modern-teachers-and-reform.md) — (Tiruvaruṭpā; Wikipedia (Ramalinga Swamigal)) [scholarly]
- **Ramana Maharshi renders the Dṛg-Dṛśya-Viveka into Tamil** — the little seer-seen manual gains a wide modern readership through his Tamil prose translation, 20th c. CE _(actors: Ramana Maharshi)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/darshanas/vedanta-prakarana-granthas.md) — (Dṛg-Dṛśya-Viveka, Ramanasramam editions)
- **Ramaṇa Maharṣi revives the Tripurā Rahasya** — praising the Jñāna-khaṇḍa as a supreme work on Self-inquiry, Ramaṇa Maharṣi has it translated into English at Sri Ramanasramam, carrying the once-obscure Śākta saṃhitā into the modern Advaita revival _(actors: Ramaṇa Maharṣi, Munagala Venkataramaiah)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/tripura-rahasya.md) — (Sri Ramanasramam; Talks with Sri Ramana Maharshi) [scholarly]
- **Rāmānanda opens Rāma-bhakti at Vārāṇasī** — c. 1400 CE Rāmānanda teaches Rāma-devotion across caste lines, seeding the Rāmānandī sampradāya, among the largest Vaiṣṇava monastic orders [scholarly] _(actors: Rāmānanda, Kabīr)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/characters/rama.md) — (Rāmānandī tradition)
- **Rāmānuja quotes the Keśidhvaja discourse** — the Śrīvaiṣṇava ācārya (c. 1017–1137 CE) cites Viṣṇu Purāṇa VI.5's definition of Bhagavat and VI.7's self-as-knowledge (jñāna-svarūpa) as proof-texts of Viśiṣṭādvaita _(actors: Rāmānuja, Parāśara)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/khandikya-keshidhvaja.md) — (Śrī-Bhāṣya; Vedārtha-saṅgraha) [scholarly]
- **Rāmcaritmānas begun at Ayodhyā** — Tulsīdās begins his Awadhi epic of Rāma on Rāma-navamī, V.S. 1631 (1574 CE), completing it in about two years and seven months across Ayodhyā, Kāśī and Citrakūṭ _(actors: Tulsīdās)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/tulsidas.md) — (Wikipedia, Ramcharitmanas [scholarly])
- **Ratnāvalī's rebuke and Tulsī's renunciation** — his wife Ratnāvalī shames the infatuated Tulsī ("had you such love for Rāma, you would have no fear of rebirth") and he renounces household life on the spot _(actors: Tulsīdās, Ratnāvalī)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/tulsidas.md) — (Mūla Gosāīṃ Carita [folk])
- **Rebirth from the fire as Bhagavatī** — by Śiva's grace the maiden rises out of the flames as a goddess and wanders the earth seeking a place to be received _(actors: the reborn Bhagavatī, Śiva)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/muchilottu-bhagavathy.md) — (Muchilottu Bhagavathy thoṭṭam-pāṭṭu)
- **Reborn as the spirit-children Nīli and Nīlaṉ** — the murdered wife and her dead brother return as uncanny cattle-killing infants, abandoned tied to a karuvēl tree in the wilds of Pañcavaṉkāḍu _(actors: Nīli, Nīlaṉ)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/icakki-amman-nili-katai.md) — (Nīli Katai)
- **Rescue of Lachha Gujari's cattle** — Teja pursues the raiders of Chang alone, recovers the herdswoman Lachha's whole herd, and is wounded over his entire body [folk] _(actors: Tejaji, Lachha Gujari, Minas of Chang)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/tejaji.md) — (Tejaji katha)
- **Return after twelve years** — his lifespan secured by yoga, Gopīcandra returns to Rangpur and to the enduring Aduna and Paduna, the prophecy vindicated as the king cheats death by having become a beggar _(actors: Gopīcandra, Aduna, Paduna, Maynāmatī)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/gopichandra-maynamati-nath-epic.md) — (Gopīcandrer Gān) [folk]
- **Roop Kanwar and the Commission of Sati (Prevention) Act (1987)** — The immolation of 18-year-old Roop Kanwar at Deorala and the mass glorification that followed prompt a law criminalizing both abetment and glorification of satī [scholarly] _(actors: Roop Kanwar, Government of India)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/sati-jauhar-and-the-immolation-rites.md) — (Commission of Sati (Prevention) Act, 1987)
- **Rukmini Devi founds Kalakshetra and renames sadir 'Bharatanāṭyam'** — the revival re-frames the devadāsī art as a spiritualized concert form (1936 CE) [scholarly/disputed] _(actors: Rukmini Devi Arundale, E. Krishna Iyer, T. Balasaraswati)_ → [details](../09-artifacts-symbols-and-arts/classical-dance-forms-and-ritual-theatre.md) — (Modern dance-history scholarship)
- **RV 10.85 codified as the vivaha liturgy** — the Grihya-sutras fix Suryaa's wedding verses (the hand-clasp, the samrajni blessing) as the core of the Hindu marriage rite, mid-1st millennium BCE [scholarly] _(actors: Grihya-sutra ritualists)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/vedas/rigveda-samvada-and-secular-hymns.md) — (Grihya-sutras on Rigveda 10.85)
- **Sābarṇa Roy Choudhury family pūjā begins (1610 CE)** — the Barisha (Behala) household pūjā starts and runs continuously, commonly counted the oldest extant Durgā Pūjā of the Kolkata region _(actors: Sābarṇa Roy Choudhury family)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/durga-puja-bengal.md) — (family and civic records) [scholarly]
- **Sadānanda Yogīndra writes the Vedāntasāra** — the standard compact prose introduction to Advaita (anubandha-catuṣṭaya, adhyāropa-apavāda, bhāga-tyāga reading of tat tvam asi), c. 15th c. CE [scholarly] _(actors: Sadānanda Yogīndra)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/darshanas/vedanta-prakarana-granthas.md) — (Vedāntasāra)
- **Sadāśiva Brahmendra, the silent avadhūta** — the 18th-c. jīvanmukta of the Kāverī (samādhi at Nerur) writes the Ātma-vidyā-vilāsa and Yoga-sudhākara and composes the Sanskrit Carnatic kṛtis Manasa sañcara re and Pibare rāma-rasam _(actors: Sadāśiva Brahmendra, Paramaśivendra Sarasvatī)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/later-advaita-acharyas.md) — (Ātma-vidyā-vilāsa; Wikipedia) [folk]
- **Sadukti-karṇāmṛta anthologizes Jayadeva (1205 CE)** — Śrīdharadāsa's Sena-court anthology cites the poet, the earliest external anchor for his date and fame [scholarly] _(actors: Śrīdharadāsa, Jayadeva)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/jayadeva-and-the-gita-govinda.md) — (Sadukti-karṇāmṛta, 1205 CE)
- **Śākambharī of Sambhar** — the Goddess becomes kuladevī of the Cāhamāna (Chauhan) dynasty, whose capital Śākambharī (Sambhar, Rajasthan) is named for her, ruling from there c. 6th–12th c. CE _(actors: Śākambharī Devī, the Cāhamānas)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/shakambhari-shatakshi-durgamasura.md) — ([scholarly]; Cāhamāna tradition)
- **Śākambharī Pūrṇimā cult** — the living Śākambharī cult at Saharanpur, Sambhar and Badami (Banaśaṅkarī) observes the eight-day Śākambharī Navarātri and Pauṣa-full-moon Śākambharī Jayantī, offering vegetables to the food-giving Goddess _(actors: Śākambharī Devī, Bhīmā, Bhrāmarī)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/shakambhari-shatakshi-durgamasura.md) — ([regional]; living tradition)
- **Sākṣī-Gopāla carried north** — on the same southern campaign Puruṣottama Deva brings the Witness-Kṛṣṇa deity Sākṣī-Gopāla from the Vidyānagara country to Odisha, enshrined near Purī _(actors: Puruṣottama Deva, Sākṣī-Gopāla)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/kanchi-kaveri-jagannatha-legend.md) — (Odia / Gauḍīya temple tradition) [regional] [disputed]
- **Sālabega the yavana poet turns to Jagannātha** — the Muslim subedār's son, healed of a mortal battle-wound after his Brāhmaṇ mother teaches him to call on Jagannātha, renounces arms to become Odisha's great bhajan-poet, barred from the temple yet singing 'Āhe Nīla Śaila' _(actors: Sālabega, his mother, Jagannātha)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/characters/jagannatha-bhakta-legends.md) — (Odia bhakta tradition; Sālabega's bhajans, 17th c.) [regional] [scholarly]
- **Salhesh and Dinā-Bhadrī mobilised for Dalit assertion in Bihar (late 20th c.)** — The hero-cults are consciously re-purposed as icons of Dusādh and Musahar identity politics — statues, memorial melās and printed hero-pamphlets — documented by Badri Narayan _(actors: Musahar and Dusādh communities, Badri Narayan (documenter))_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/maithili-oral-epics-salhesh-dina-bhadri.md) — (Badri Narayan, Women Heroes and Dalit Assertion in North India, 2006) [scholarly]
- **Salhesh deified as protector-god of the Dusādhs** — The vindicated watchman-king is worshipped across Mithilā at gahwar shrines heaped with terracotta elephants, where possessed bhagat priest-mediums sing his gāthā and give healing and judgement _(actors: Rājā Salhesh, Dusādh community, bhagat mediums)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/maithili-oral-epics-salhesh-dina-bhadri.md) — (Salhesh gahwar tradition of Mithilā) [folk]
- **Salhesh falsely accused of the palace jewel theft** — Rājā Salhesh of Mahisautha, serving as watchman of a royal fort, is framed for the theft of the royal jewels during his watch and imprisoned _(actors: Rājā Salhesh, the royal court, Daunā Mālin (variant))_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/maithili-oral-epics-salhesh-dina-bhadri.md) — (Rājā Salhes gāthā) [oral tradition]
- **Samadhi of Dali Bai** — Dali Bai takes samadhi shortly before Ramdev; her tomb-shrine beside his at Ramdevra is by custom venerated first by pilgrims _(actors: Dali Bai, Ramdev)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/ramdev-pir.md) — (Ramdevra sthala tradition) [regional]
- **Sambandar's Madurai contest (7th c. CE)** — the child-saint cures the fevered Pāṇḍya and defeats the Jains in ordeals of fire and flood; the impalement of 8,000 Jains is doubted as history [disputed] _(actors: Sambandar, Ninṟacīr Neṭumāṟaṉ, Maṅkaiyarkkaraci, Kulacciṟai)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/periya-puranam-63-nayanmar-legend-map.md) — (Periya Purāṇam, Tiruñāṉacampantar Purāṇam)
- **Sammakka's stand at Medaram and the vermilion casket** — the Koya tiger-forest foundling-queen fights the Kakatiya tax-army at Sampenga Vagu, then vanishes into Chilakala Gutta leaving only a vermilion kumkuma casket and a promise to return every two years — origin of the biennial Medaram jatara, India's largest tribal religious gathering _(actors: Sammakka, Sāralamma, Jampanna, Kakatiya army)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/gramadevatas-village-deities.md) — (Koya oral tradition [folk])
- **Śāṇḍilya-vidyā taught in the Chāndogya Upaniṣad** — the Vedic seer behind the sūtra-ascription teaches "sarvaṁ khalv idaṁ brahma," c. 800–600 BCE [scholarly] _(actors: Śāṇḍilya)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/bhakti-sutras-narada-shandilya.md) — (Chāndogya Upaniṣad, 3.14)
- **Śaṅkara at the Śāradā throne** — Kashmir's Śāradā Pīṭha flourishes as a manuscript-university on the Kishangangā, its sarvajña-pīṭha ascended in tradition by Ādi Śaṅkara, c. 8th–12th c. CE _(actors: Śāradā/Sarasvatī, Ādi Śaṅkara)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/kashmir-tirthas-nilamata-geography.md) — (Śāradā-deśa tradition; hagiographies) [scholarly]
- **Śaṅkara composes the Upadeśa Sāhasrī** — the one non-commentarial work of broadly accepted Śaṅkara authorship, a two-part (verse + prose) manual of direct Advaita instruction, c. 700–750 CE [scholarly] _(actors: Adi Shankara)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/darshanas/vedanta-prakarana-granthas.md) — (Upadeśa Sāhasrī; Mayeda, A Thousand Teachings)
- **Śaṅkara encounters the aged grammarian in Kāśī** — Ādi Śaṅkara, walking Vārāṇasī with his disciples, comes upon an old Brahmin drilling Pāṇinian grammar (ḍukṛñ-karaṇe) as death nears _(actors: Ādi Śaṅkara, the aged grammarian, Śaṅkara's disciples)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/stotras/bhaja-govindam.md) — (Bhaja Govindam / Mohamudgara frame-story; Śaṅkara-vijaya hagiographies)
- **Śaṅkara encounters the old grammarian in Kāśī** — Walking the lanes of Vārāṇasī with his disciples, Ādi Śaṅkara comes upon an aged Brahmin grammarian rote-drilling the Pāṇinian rule 'ḍukṛñ-karaṇe' as death nears him _(actors: Ādi Śaṅkara, his disciples, an unnamed old Brahmin grammarian)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/stotras/bhaja-govindam.md) — (Bhaja Govindam (traditional frame); Śaṅkara-vijaya hagiographies)
- **Andal Discovered Beneath the Tulasi** — The Alvar Periyalvar finds the infant girl Kodai (Andal) under a tulasi plant in the Srivilliputhur temple garden and raises her in Krishna-devotion, her earth-birth underwriting her Bhu-devi identity _(actors: Periyalvar, Andal)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/andal.md) — (Andal — Wikipedia)
- **Basava rejects the sacred thread** — As a boy Basava rejects the Brahminical sacred thread (upanayana) in protest at birth-based hierarchy and leaves for Kudalasangama to study, taking Kudalasangamadeva as his form of Shiva _(actors: Basava)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/lingayat-vachana-poets.md) — (Basava hagiography (traditional))
- **Bhagavata announcement of the Buddha-avatara** — Vishnu foretold to descend as Buddha, son of Anjana, in Kikata (Magadha) at the start of Kali-yuga to delude the enemies of the faithful _(actors: Vishnu, Buddha, Anjana)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/buddha-avatara.md) — (Bhagavata Purana 1.3.24, 1.3.26)
- **Birth and identity of Bahiṇābāī** — A Deśastha Brāhmaṇa woman, c. 1628–1700 CE, becomes a Vārkarī sant, distinct from the later 1880–1951 Khandeshi folk-poet of the same name _(actors: Bahiṇābāī)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/bahinabai.md) — (Wikipedia 'Bahina Bai'; Feldhaus, Bahina Bai: A Translation; Tharu & Lalita, Women Writing in India)
- **Birth and life of Eknāth at Paithan** — Eknāth, a Deśastha Brāhmaṇa householder-saint descended from Bhānudās, lives at Paithan on the Godāvarī as the pivotal mid-tradition Vārkarī figure (c. 1533–1599 CE) _(actors: Eknāth, Bhānudās (ancestor), wife Girijābāī, son Hari Paṇḍit)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/eknath.md) — (Wikipedia Eknath; Britannica; Vārkarī hagiography)
- **Birth and marriage of Puṉitavati** — Born Puṉitavati, devout daughter of merchant Taṉatattaṉ of Kāraikkāl, married to the merchant's son Paramatattaṉ _(actors: Puṉitavati, Taṉatattaṉ, Paramatattaṉ)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/karaikkal-ammaiyar.md) — (Periya Purāṇam (Cēkkiḻār))
- **Birth and milk legend of Sambandar** — Born at Sirkazhi to a Brahmin couple, the toddler Sambandar is fed divine milk by Pārvatī from a golden cup and sings his first hymn, earning his name _(actors: Sambandar, Pārvatī)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/the-nalvar-tamil-shaiva-saints.md) — (Wikipedia, Sambandar)
- **Birth and traditional dating of Sūrdās** — Sūrdās, blind Braj-bhāṣā poet, born (trad. c. 1478 CE, near Delhi/Braj); scholarship favors an earlier, shorter 16th-c. floruit _(actors: Sūrdās)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/surdas.md) — (Wikipedia Surdas; Britannica; Hawley; Caurāsī Vaiṣṇavan kī Vārtā)
- **Birth as Nārāyaṇ at Jāmb** — Born Nārāyaṇ Ṭhosar c. 1608 at Jāmb in Marathwada to a Deśastha Brahmin sun-worshipping family _(actors: Nārāyaṇ (Rāmdās), Sūryājipant Ṭhosar, Rāṇūbāī, brother Gaṅgādhar)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/samarth-ramdas.md) — (Wikipedia (Samarth Ramdas); Hindupedia)
- **Birth at Gangākheḍ and placement in Nāmdev's household** — Janābāī is born to low-caste Vārkarī parents Dāmā and Karuṇḍ at Gangākheḍ on the Godāvarī and, after her mother's death, is placed as a maidservant (dāsī) in Nāmdev's Pandharpur household _(actors: Janābāī, Dāmā, Karuṇḍ, Nāmdev's family)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/janabai.md) — (Wikipedia (Janabai); Vārkarī hagiography)
- **Birth of Śaṅkaradeva** — Śrīmanta Śaṅkaradeva, founder of Ekaśaraṇa Dharma, is born into a Bhuyan Kāyastha family at Bordowā/Batadrava in Nagaon, Assam (traditionally 1449) _(actors: Śaṅkaradeva)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/sankaradeva-and-assamese-vaishnavism.md) — (Wikipedia, Sankardev)
- **Birth of Bonbibi and Shah Jangali in Arabia** — Bonbibi and her twin brother Shah Jangali are born to the fakir Ibrahim and his second wife Gulalbibi and abandoned in the forest, where Bonbibi is suckled by a deer _(actors: Bonbibi, Shah Jangali, Ibrahim, Gulalbibi)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/bonbibi.md) — (Bonbibi Jahuranama)
- **Birth of Nammalvar and the subduing of Shatha** — Born Maran to a non-Brahmin Vellalar family, the infant Nammalvar subdues Shatha (the wind afflicting newborns), earning the title Shathakopa, and never cries, eats or speaks _(actors: Nammalvar, Kari, Udaya Nankai)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/nammalvar.md) — (Nammalvar hagiography (traditional))
- **Birth of Nāmdev into the śimpī community** — Nāmdev is born (c. 1270) into the low-caste śimpī tailor/calico-printer community of Pandharpur, into a Viṭṭhal-devotee household _(actors: Nāmdev, Dāmāśeṭī (father), Goṇāī (mother))_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/namdev.md) — (Wikipedia Namdev; Vārkarī hagiography)
- **Birth of Ravidās at Seer Govardhanpur** — Ravidās is born into a Camār (leather-worker / Dalit) family in a village on the edge of Vārāṇasī, c. 1450 CE (traditional) _(actors: Ravidās, parents Santokh Dās and Kalsā)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/ravidas.md) — (Wikipedia Ravidas; Grokipedia Guru Ravidass Jayanti)
- **Birth of the four prodigy siblings** — Viṭṭhalapant and Rukmiṇībāī's four children — Nivṛttināth, Jñāneśvar, Sopāndev, Muktābāī — born c. 1273–1279 at Āpegāv/Āḷandī, all later venerated as saints _(actors: Viṭṭhalapant, Rukmiṇībāī, Nivṛttināth, Jñāneśvar, Sopāndev, Muktābāī)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/nivritti-sopan-muktabai.md) — (Wikipedia (Nivruttinath, Sopan, Muktabai, Dnyaneshwar))
- **Birth to the courtesan Śyāmā of Mangalveḍhā** — Kānhopātrā is born to Śyāmā, a wealthy courtesan of Mangalvedha, and raised in song and dance _(actors: Kānhopātrā, Śyāmā (her courtesan mother))_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/kanhopatra.md) — (Vārkarī hagiography)
- **Boy Śaṅkara on bhikṣā rounds** — The young brahmacārin Śaṅkara goes house to house begging alms-food as a student-ascetic _(actors: Ādi Śaṅkara (as a boy))_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/stotras/kanakadhara-stotram.md) — (Kanakadhārā-stotra frame; Śaṅkara-vijaya hagiographies)
- **Brāhmaṇa birth and child-marriage** — Lallā is born to a Kashmiri Brāhmaṇa family near Srinagar and married young into a household at Pampore _(actors: Lalleśvarī (Lallā); her natal Brāhmaṇa family; marital household at Pampore)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/lalleshwari-lal-ded.md) — (Wikipedia, Lalleshwari; Kashmiri tradition)
- **Chandan Yatra (Chandana Yatra)** — The 21-day sandal-paste boat festival opening the Puri Jagannath ritual year on Akshaya Tritiya: the representative deities Madanmohan (with Sri Devi and Bhu Devi) and the Pancha-Pandavas are rowed on decorated boats (chapas) on the Narendra tank and anointed with cooling sandal-paste against the summer heat; the same day chariot-building for the Rath Yatra begins _(actors: Madanmohan (chalanti Jagannath), Sri Devi, Bhu Devi, Pancha-Pandavas (five Shiva lingas))_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/regional-temple-festivals.md) — (Wikipedia (Chandan Yatra); Jagannath Temple Puri [traditional])
- **Childless couple's prayer for a child** — The childless Brahmin illam-couple Ayyankara Vazhunnavar and Padikutty Amma perform austerities praying for a son _(actors: Ayyankara Vazhunnavar, Padikutty Amma)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/muthappan.md) — (Parassinikkadavu Muthappan origin legend (theyyam oral tradition))
- **Chokhamela born a Mahar at Pandharpur** — The first-tier Varkari poet-saint Chokhamela is born into the untouchable Mahar community at Pandharpur, a devotee of Vithoba _(actors: Chokhamela)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/chokhamela.md) — (Wikipedia (Chokhamela); Varkari hagiography; Zelliot, From Untouchable to Dalit)
- **Codification of the shared temple vocabulary (garbhagṛha, maṇḍapa, antarāla, vimāna)** — The śilpa/vāstu corpus fixes the functional parts of the Hindu temple — womb-sanctum, pillared halls, vestibule, circumambulatory, plinth, crowning tower — as a single shared grammar laid on the vāstu-puruṣa-maṇḍala _(actors: Śilpins/sthapatis; the architect-god Viśvakarmā (mythic source); Maya (asura-architect))_ → [details](../09-artifacts-symbols-and-arts/temple-architecture-nagara-dravida-vesara.md) — (Mānasāra; Mayamata; Bṛhat Saṃhitā; Wikipedia (Hindu temple architecture, Garbhagriha, Mandapa))
- **Composition of the Hanuman Chalisa by Tulsidas** — Goswami Tulsidas composes a forty-caupai Awadhi hymn to Hanuman as a vernacular devotional companion to his Ramcharitmanas _(actors: Goswami Tulsidas)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/stotras/hanuman-chalisa.md) — (Hanuman Chalisa (Awadhi); Tulsidas tradition)
- **Composition of the Kakawin Ramayana** — The oldest major Southeast Asian Rama text is composed in Central Java c. 856-930 CE, modeled on the Sanskrit Bhattikavya rather than Valmiki directly _(actors: Anonymous Old Javanese poet (sometimes ascribed to Yogesvara))_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/ramayana/recensions/southeast-asian-ramayanas.md) — (Kakawin Ramayana)
- **Composition of the Nirvana Shatkam by Adi Shankara** — Adi Shankara composes the six-verse Atma Shatkam distilling nirguna Brahman and neti-neti into the refrain Chidananda-rupah Shivo'ham _(actors: Adi Shankaracarya)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/stotras/nirvana-shatkam.md) — (Nirvana Shatkam / Atma Shatkam (attr. Adi Shankara))
- **Composition of the Saundarya-lahari** — The 100-verse Sri Vidya hymn to Tripurasundari is composed/transmitted in the mature medieval Shakta-Tantra stratum, traditionally ascribed to Adi Shankara _(actors: Adi Shankara (traditional attribution); Sri Vidya tradition)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/stotras/soundarya-lahari.md) — (Saundarya-lahari (attrib. Adi Shankara))
- **Composition of the Venkatesha Suprabhatam** — The Sri Vaishnava acharya Prativadi Bhayankaram Annan composes the four-part dawn-awakening sequence for Lord Venkateshvara of Tirumala _(actors: Prativadi Bhayankaram Annan (Annangaracharya))_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/stotras/venkatesha-suprabhatam.md) — (Venkatesha Suprabhatam (15th c.); Sri Vaishnava acharya tradition)
- **Construction of Rājarājeśvaram (the Big Temple)** — Rājarāja Chola I builds and consecrates the Thanjavur Big Temple c. 1010 CE, naming it after himself _(actors: Rājarāja Chola I; the architect Kunjara Mallan Rāja Rāja Perunthachan)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/brihadeeswarar-thanjavur.md) — (Chola inscriptions of Rājarāja I)
- **Cosmetic and decorative kalās catalogued in the Kāmasūtra** — Vātsyāyana lists body-adornment skills (Viśeṣaka-cchedya, Aṅga-rāga, Citra-yoga, Bhūṣaṇa-yojana) among the sixty-four arts, the classical anchor for ritual body-painting _(actors: Vātsyāyana)_ → [details](../09-artifacts-symbols-and-arts/mehndi-and-ritual-body-adornment.md) — (Kāmasūtra 1.3 (Vidyā-samuddeśa))
- **Crystallization of the Nāth Sampradāya** — The Śaiva Siddha-yogī order coalesces in the 12th–13th c. CE from earlier Pāśupata, Kāpālika, Kaula and Siddha cults, with Śiva (Ādinātha) as first guru _(actors: Ādinātha (Śiva), Nāth yogīs)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/nath-sampradaya-and-the-siddhas.md) — (Wikipedia (Natha Sampradaya); Britannica (Gorakhnath); Gorakṣa-siddhānta-saṅgraha)
- **Ādi Śaṅkara installs Śāradā at Śṛṅgerī** — On his digvijaya tour, Ādi Śaṅkara chooses Śṛṅgerī on the Tuṅgā river for the southern Śāradā Pīṭha and consecrates the goddess of learning as a sandalwood (candana) mūrti, founding the first of his four cardinal Veda-seats and making Śāradāmbā the presiding deity of the southern Śaṅkarācārya lineage _(actors: Ādi Śaṅkara, Śāradā/Śāradāmbā)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/saraswati.md) — (Madhavīya Śaṅkara-Digvijaya; Śṛṅgerī Maṭha tradition)
- **Dolā Yātrā / Dol Pūrṇimā** — On Phālguna Pūrṇimā, Kṛṣṇa as Dolagovinda (with Rādhā/Lakṣmī) is rocked on a decorated swing-palanquin amid colored powder — the Vaiṣṇava counterpart to north-Indian Holi _(actors: Kṛṣṇa/Jagannāth as Dolagovinda-Madanmohan, Rādhā, devotees)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/swing-and-float-temple-festivals.md) — (Bhāgavata Kṛṣṇa-līlā substrate; Odisha Jagannāth ritual calendar; Wikipedia (Dol Purnima); Drik Panchang)
- **Gorakhnāth's boon and Gugga's miraculous birth** — The barren Chauhan queen Bāchhal pleases the Nāth siddha Gorakhnāth, who grants a guggal-fruit prasād by which she conceives the serpent-favored hero Gugga _(actors: Bāchhal (Bacchal Devī), Guru Gorakhnāth, infant Gugga)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/gugga-gogaji.md) — (Rajasthani/Haryanvi Gugga-gāthā)
- **Gorā Kumbhār's life and abhaṅgas at Ter** — The potter-saint 'Gorobā Kākā' of Ter lives as a Viṭhobā-devotee in the founding Jñāneśvar–Nāmdev Vārkarī cohort, composing Marathi abhaṅgas drawn from the potter's craft _(actors: Gorā Kumbhār, Jñāneśvar, Nāmdev)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/gora-kumbhar.md) — (Wikipedia, Gora Kumbhar; Vārkarī abhaṅga tradition)
- **Gugga deified as snakebite-protector god** — Gugga (Gogājī / Jāharvīr Gogā), a Cauhāṇ-Rajput warrior-chieftain of the Hindi-Rajasthani belt (c. 11th-12th c. CE), is deified after death as the supreme snakebite-protector deity of the northwest, worshipped by Hindus as Gogājī and Muslims as Zāhir/Jāhar Pīr at the Gogāmeṛī shrine, celebrated at Gogā Navamī and sung in the Gugga-gāthā / Gugga kī ghoṛī cycle _(actors: Gugga/Gogājī (Jāharvīr Gogā, Zāhir Pīr); mother Bāchal; cousins Arjan-Surjan)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/regional-and-oral-epics.md) — (Gugga-gāthā / Gugga kī ghoṛī (oral bardic cycle); Goga (deity) — Wikipedia)
- **Indus Valley / Harappan Civilization** — Mature urban Harappan civilization flourishes c. 2600-1900 BCE with planned cities, standardized weights, and an undeciphered script, with disputed claims linking it to Vedic culture _(actors: Harappan civilization)_ → [details](../01-manvantaras/manvantara-07-vaivasvata/mahayuga-28-current/04-kali-yuga/recorded-history.md) — (History of India (Wikipedia); archaeology consensus)
- **Jagannath Rath Yatra (charter procession)** — Jagannath, Balabhadra, Subhadra and Sudarshana Chakra are pulled in three towering chariots from the Puri temple to the Gundicha Temple and back, the annual chariot festival of the Char Dham _(actors: Jagannath, Balabhadra, Subhadra)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/regional-temple-festivals.md) — (Wikipedia, Ratha Yatra (Puri); Britannica)
- **Kabir found as an infant on Lahartara Lake** — A Brahmana widow abandons the infant Kabir on Lahartara Lake in Varanasi, where the Muslim weaver couple Niru and Nima find and raise him, making him Hindu by blood and Muslim by upbringing _(actors: Kabir, Niru, Nima, Brahmana widow)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/kabir.md) — (Kabir hagiography (folk))
- **Karuppasāmi as Ayyaṉār's black gatekeeper** — Karuppasāmi, the black guardian-god with aruvāḷ iconography, serves as chief lieutenant and threshold-gatekeeper of Ayyaṉār in the kāval cult _(actors: Karuppasāmi, Ayyaṉār)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/karuppasamy-madurai-veeran-and-tamil-kaval-deities.md) — (Ayyaṉār-Karuppu kāval cult tradition; Tamil kāval-deyvam tradition)
- **Kulashekhara renounces his Chera throne for devotion** — The Chera king-saint Kulasekhara Alvar abandons kingship and worldly desire for absorption in Vishnu, the royal-renunciation frame of the hymn _(actors: Kulasekhara Alvar (Chera king))_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/stotras/mukunda-mala.md) — (Mukunda-mala Stotra; Perumal Tirumozhi; Alvar hagiography)
- **Lagadha's Vedāṅga Jyotiṣa** — Oldest extant Indian astronomical text, a ritual luni-solar calendar tracking Sun and Moon against the nakṣatras, with no zodiac _(actors: Lagadha)_ → [details](../00-time-and-cosmology/siddhantic-astronomy-and-astronomers.md) — (Vedāṅga Jyotiṣa; histories of Indian astronomy)
- **Lakulīśa reforms the Pāśupata order** — The Gujarat sage Lakulīśa (~2nd c. CE), regarded as an incarnation of Śiva, reforms the Pāśupata movement and authors the foundational Pāśupata-sūtra, renaming it Lākula-Pāśupata _(actors: Lakulīśa)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/shaiva-schools.md) — (Wikipedia, Pashupata Shaivism)
- **Manasā born of Śiva (motherless from Pārvatī)** — Śiva's seed/will quickens a lotus or clay snake-image in the netherworld, producing the serpent-goddess Manasā without Pārvatī _(actors: Śiva, Manasā)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/manasa.md) — (Manasāmaṅgal (Bijay Gupta, Ketakādāsa))
- **Mirabai's childhood vow to Krishna** — As a child before an image of Krishna, Mira declares 'He is my husband,' making her later earthly marriage a formality over a prior spiritual vow to Giridhar _(actors: Mirabai, Krishna)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/mirabai.md) — (Mirabai hagiography)
- **Mirkhshah's persecution of the Sindhi Hindus** — The ruler Mirkhshah of Thatta threatens the Sindhi Hindus with forced conversion, granting forty days to decide _(actors: Mirkhshah, the Sindhi Hindu community)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/jhulelal.md) — (Sindhi folk tradition (Uderolal/Jhulelal sakhi))
- **Māri as rain-and-death mother** — Māriyammaṉ worshipped as the Tamil grāmadevatā who simultaneously sends and cures pox/epidemic and brings the monsoon rain _(actors: Māriyammaṉ; Tamil villagers)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/mariamman.md) — (Tamil folk tradition; grāmadevatā studies)
- **Naraharitīrtha pioneers Kannada devaranāma** — Earliest recognized progenitor of Haridāsa song within Madhva's paramparā, reputed pioneer of Kannada devaranāma; fl. late 13th–early 14th c _(actors: Naraharitīrtha)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/haridasa-and-carnatic-bhakti.md) — (Haridāsa-sāhitya; Wikipedia, Narahari Tirtha)
- **Nathamuni Recovers the Naalayira Divya Prabandham** — Nathamuni (c. 824-924 CE) recovers the lost 4,000 Alvar hymns by reciting Madhurakavi's verses until Nammalvar reveals the whole corpus, compiling and setting it to music as the Dravida Veda _(actors: Nathamuni, Nammalvar, Madhurakavi)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/alvars-and-nayanars.md) — (Naalayira Divya Prabandham)
- **Nimbarka traps the sun in a neem tree** — To honor a sannyasi guest after sunset when ascetics may not eat, Nimbarka traps the setting sun (arka) in a neem (nimba) tree so its light lingers, earning his name _(actors: Nimbarka, a sannyasi guest)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/nimbarka-vallabha-chaitanya.md) — (Nimbarka hagiography (folk))
- **Pañca-loha & aṣṭadhātu codified as sacred image-alloys** — The Śilpa-Śāstra tradition fixes the five/eight-metal alloys (copper-base with gold, silver, zinc, lead/iron) as canonical for dhātu-pratimā, keyed to deity _(actors: śilpin / sthapati craftsmen; Śilpa-Śāstra redactors)_ → [details](../09-artifacts-symbols-and-arts/pancha-loha-bronze-casting.md) — (Mānasāra; Mayamata; Śukranīti 4)
- **Pañch Kailāsh grouping of five Kailāsa peaks** — Five Śaiva Kailāsa summits — Tibet's Mount Kailāsa plus four Indian Himalayan peaks — assembled into a single devotional pilgrimage pentad paralleling the Pañcha Kedār/Badrī fives _(actors: Śaiva pilgrims; Śiva as mountain-abode deity)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/panch-kailash.md) — (Wikipedia (Panch Kailash); regional yātrā tradition; Skanda Purāṇa & Śiva Purāṇa)
- **Patañjali compiles the Yoga Sūtras** — Patañjali composes/redacts the 196-sūtra foundational manual of classical Yoga (c. 2nd c. BCE - 5th c. CE), crystallising Sāṃkhya-with-Īśvara as the practical darśana of stilling the mind _(actors: Patañjali)_ → [details](../04-deep-dives/yoga-sutras-of-patanjali.md) — (Yoga Sūtras of Patañjali)
- **Pālegāḍu terrorises Tirupati** — A predatory local chieftain (Pālegāḍu) menaces Tirupati and its women, provoking Gangamma's resolve to destroy him _(actors: Gangamma, the Pālegāḍu (chieftain), women of Tirupati)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/gangamma.md) — (Tirupati Gangamma Jātara oral tradition [folk])
- **Puṇḍalīka tosses the brick** — The devoted son Puṇḍalīka, absorbed in serving his aged parents, tosses a brick for the arriving Lord to stand and wait on, fixing Viṭṭhal's standing pose at Pandharpur _(actors: Puṇḍalīka (Puṇḍarīka), Viṭṭhal/Kṛṣṇa-Viṣṇu)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/vithoba-vitthal.md) — (Pāṇḍuraṅga-māhātmya (Skanda Purāṇa / Padma Purāṇa); Vārkarī hagiography)
- **Ramananda founds the Ramanandi Sampradaya** — Ramananda pioneers the organization of north-Indian bhakti and founds the Ramanandi (Ramavat) ascetic order centred on Rama-Sita, accepting disciples regardless of caste, gender or creed and preaching in vernacular Hindi _(actors: Ramananda)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/north-bhakti-sants.md) — (Bhaktamal tradition)
- **Ram Mohan Roy founds the Brahmo Sabha** — Raja Ram Mohan Roy founds the Brahmo Sabha at Calcutta on 20 August 1828, the rationalist monotheist precursor of the Brahmo Samaj and engine of the Bengal Renaissance _(actors: Ram Mohan Roy, Dwarkanath Tagore)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/modern-teachers-and-reform.md) — (Brahmo Samaj history)
- **Rise of the aniconic Dharma Ṭhākur cult in the Rāṛh** — The aniconic stone/tortoise god Dharma Ṭhākur emerges as a non-Brahmanical justice-and-fertility deity of lower-caste communities in western Bengal _(actors: Dharma Ṭhākur (Dharmarāj), Ḍom/Bāuri/Bāgdi devotees, Ḍom paṇḍit/deāsī priests)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/dharma-thakur.md) — (Bengali folk and ritual tradition of the Rāṛh)
- **Śaṅkaradeva creates the Sattriya complex** — aṅkīyā nāṭ plays, bhāonā staging and monastery dance in the Assamese sattras (1449–1568 CE) [scholarly] _(actors: Śrīmanta Śaṅkaradeva, the sattra monks)_ → [details](../09-artifacts-symbols-and-arts/classical-dance-forms-and-ritual-theatre.md) — (Ekaśaraṇa tradition)
- **Sāraḷā Dāsa composes the first complete vernacular Mahābhārata** — the 'Śūdra Muni' of Jhankada writes the 18-parva Odia epic under Gajapati Kapilendra Deva, c. 15th century CE [scholarly] _(actors: Sāraḷā Dāsa, goddess Sāraḷā Caṇḍī, Kapilendra Deva)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/odia-literature-sarala-and-panchasakha.md) — (Sāraḷā Mahābhārata; Odia literary histories)
- **Sattriya recognized as the eighth classical dance** — the Sangeet Natak Akademi completes the modern canon of eight forms (2000 CE) [scholarly] _(actors: Sangeet Natak Akademi)_ → [details](../09-artifacts-symbols-and-arts/classical-dance-forms-and-ritual-theatre.md) — (Sangeet Natak Akademi records)
- **Sax's Mountain Goddess published (1991) [scholarly]** — William S. Sax's ethnography becomes the standard academic account of Nandā Devī, the dhiyāṇī theology and the Rāj Jāt _(actors: William S. Sax)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/nanda-devi-and-uttarakhand-deities.md) — (Sax, Mountain Goddess, 1991)
- **Sāyaṇa's Veda-bhāṣyas** — Vidyāraṇya's brother Sāyaṇa produces the standard medieval commentaries on the Vedas, traditionally under Vidyāraṇya's patronage and direction _(actors: Sāyaṇa, Vidyāraṇya)_ → [details](../07-acharyas-and-sampradayas/later-advaita-acharyas.md) — (Sāyaṇa-bhāṣya; Wikipedia, Sayana) [scholarly]
- **Self-immolation at Karivellūr** — homeless, she stands on burning coals at the Karivellūr Śiva temple and has a passing Vaṇiya boy pour his temple oil onto the embers, dying in the blaze _(actors: the Brahmin maiden, the Vaṇiya boy)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/muchilottu-bhagavathy.md) — (Muchilottu Bhagavathy thoṭṭam-pāṭṭu)
- **She out-debates the pundits** — the maiden defeats the Perinchellūr Mūtta Gurukkaḷ and his disciples in open Vedic disputation over successive days _(actors: the Brahmin maiden, the Perinchellūr Brahmin scholars)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/muchilottu-bhagavathy.md) — (Muchilottu Bhagavathy thoṭṭam-pāṭṭu)
- **Shobhabazar Rājbāṛi pūjā with Clive as guest (1757 CE)** — after Plassey, Rājā Nabakṛṣṇa Deb mounts a spectacular palace pūjā attended by Robert Clive, template of the colonial-era zamindari-babu pūjās _(actors: Nabakṛṣṇa Deb, Robert Clive)_ → [details](../05-concepts-and-dharma/durga-puja-bengal.md) — (colonial-era Calcutta records) [scholarly]
- **Shravani Mela attested in colonial gazetteers (19th c.)** — The Sultanganj–Deoghar water-carrying fair appears in 19th-century district gazetteers as an established annual mela, the oldest documented stratum of the practice [scholarly] _(actors: Kanwariya pilgrims)_ → [details](../06-sacred-geography/kanwar-yatra-and-shravan-shiva-observances.md) — (district gazetteer tradition)
- **Siddhendra Yogī composes the Bhāmākalāpam** — the founding dance-drama of Kuchipudi, bound to the brahmin boys of Kuchipudi village; Tana Shah's copper-plate grants them the village in 1678 CE [oral tradition/scholarly] _(actors: Siddhendra Yogī, Abul Hasan Tana Shah)_ → [details](../09-artifacts-symbols-and-arts/classical-dance-forms-and-ritual-theatre.md) — (Kuchipudi village tradition; Golconda grant)
- **Sir William Jones translates Manu (1794)** — under Warren Hastings the code becomes *Institutes of Hindu Law*, elevating Manu through Kullūka's vulgate into a pillar of colonial Anglo-Hindu personal law _(actors: William Jones, Warren Hastings, Kullūka Bhaṭṭa)_ → [details](../90-literature-corpus/manusmriti-map.md) — (Manusmriti scholarship; Jones 1794)
- **Śītalā's cooling-the-heat theology** — Śītalā both sends and cools smallpox, pox, measles and fevers, the eruptions read as her presence in the body and propitiated by cooling _(actors: Śītalā Mātā; the pox-afflicted devotee)_ → [details](../02-deities-and-cosmic-hierarchy/shitala.md) — (North Indian folk tradition; vrata-kathā tradition)
